But he never went too far, never said anything that his tact could not smooth over; in fact, he was at great pains not to quarrel with the captain, like the squire had done, for the captain was evidently very sensitive within that rough exterior, as he had shown by not attempting to patch up his quarrel with the squire.

So Jerry watched them as they breakfasted in the sanded parlour of the Ship, keeping in the room all he could and dreading to be dismissed.

Presently the captain turned to him and inquired whether he had breakfasted. Jerry replied that he certainly had had a snack or two, but that broiled fish always did go down very pleasant with bread and butter and fresh milk, and accepted with alacrity the invitation from the captain to bring a chair and help himself.

===Chinese===
用饭的当儿，杰里·杰尔克（Jerry Jerk）把这两个男人细细打量了一番。上校的神情阴沉，一脸的不痛快；他心里翻来覆去掂量的，尽是他力不能解的事情——那些事压根儿超出了他的见识。辛医生（Doctor Syn）却正相反，显得极乐意谈论这些桩桩件件的怪事；只是在他那有趣、文雅、从容的谈吐底下，闷燃着一种无名的恐惧，时不时腾起一团火热的怒焰——怒的是上校在设法缉拿那个神秘的黑白混血儿（mulatto）一事上显然按兵不动。不过他从不把话说过头，从不说过一句他的乖巧抹不平的话；说实在的，他煞费苦心不与上校争执，不像乡绅那样；因为上校那副粗鲁的外表底下，分明有一根敏感的神经——他不肯想法子去同乡绅重修旧好，便是一个明证。

于是，杰里便一面看着他俩在“船”酒馆（the Ship）那间撒沙的客厅里用早餐，一面使出浑身解数赖在屋里，生怕给打发出去。

不多时，上校转过身来，问他吃过早饭没有。杰里答道，他自然是垫过一两口的，不过烤鱼就着黄油面包和鲜奶下肚，向来是顶受用的；随即欢天喜地地领了上校的邀请，搬过一把椅子，自己动手吃了起来。

===Original===
The captain got up, filled a pipe and lit it, and the Doctor did the same; then both men pushed their plates to the centre of the table, leaning their elbows on the cleared space; and Jerry in the centre, for all the world like a judge of some quaint game of skill, watched the opponents as they drew deliberately at their pipes, sending preliminary battle clouds across the table before the real tussle began—aye, a fight of brains, each one desirous of ascertaining how much the other knew or guessed about these strange events, but each very fearful of betraying what he guessed. So Jerry watched them, feeling certain that a battle was imminent, wondering upon what side he would be called to fight, and what the end of it all would be; but with all his watching and wondering he didn’t forget to eat, and eat heartily, too, for Jerry’s maxim was, “Eat when you can, and only think when you’ve got to.”

===Chinese===
上校站起身来，装了一斗烟，点上了，医生也照样来了一斗；随后两人把各自的盘子推到桌子中央，胳膊肘支在腾出来的空当上；杰里坐在正当中，活脱脱像一位古怪技艺比赛的裁判，看着这两个对手不慌不忙地吸着烟斗，在真正的角力开场之前，先朝桌面上空喷出一团团开战前的战云——是啊，这是一场脑力的搏斗，两个人都急切地想摸清对方对这些怪事知道多少、猜到几分，却又都生怕把自己的猜测泄露出去。杰里就这样瞧着他们，深信一场恶战迫在眉睫，心里直犯嘀咕：不知自己会被召到哪一边去打，也不知这一切到头来如何收场；不过瞧归瞧、想归想，他可没忘了吃，而且还吃得挺欢，因为杰里的座右铭是：“能吃的时候就吃，非动脑子不可的时候才动。”

===Original===
The captain spoke first.

“Doctor Syn, you heard me say at that inquiry yesterday that I was no strategist, that I was only a fighter.”

“I did,” returned the cleric.

“I know everything inside, outside, and around-about a ship, but I don’t know much else, and certainly nothing else thoroughly, so to speak. But I have seen other things in my time, for all that, just as anyone who travels is bound to see things, and, just as anyone else that travels, I have remembered a few things outside my business, just a few; the rest I’ve forgotten. Now you’re different from that, for you’re a scholar and have travelled widely, too, and a man who can use his book knowledge with what he comes in contact with in the world is the sort of man who might perhaps explain what’s bothering me at the present moment, for I am dense; you are not.”

“What is bothering you. Captain? Of course something to do with these murders that are uppermost in our minds?”

===Chinese===
上校先开了口。

“辛医生，昨天在查讯会上，你听我说过我这个人不懂战略，只会打仗。”

“听见过，”牧师答道。

“一条船上里里外外、前前后后的事情，我没有不通透的；可是除此以外，我就懂得不多了，而且可以说，别的没有一样算得上精通。尽管如此，我这一辈子也见过些别的事情——出门跑码头的人，总免不了要见些世面；我也同别的出门人一样，记住了几件本行以外的事，就那么几件，其余的都忘了。您可跟我不一样：您是位学者，又游历甚广；一个人若能把书本上的学问，拿来跟他在这个世界上亲身接触的东西两相参证，这样的人，兴许恰好能把我眼下这桩搅得心神不宁的事解释出来，因为我这人愚钝，您可不愚钝。”

“什么事搅得您心神不宁，上校？自然同这几桩压在我们心头忘不掉的凶案脱不了干系吧？”

===Original===
“Something, I dare say,” replied the captain slowly, weighing his every word, “but, on the other hand, maybe it’s nothing. I can’t connect the two things myself, and yet I’ve a feeling that I ought to be able to. I’ve tried, though, tried hard, been trying all through breakfast, and it worries me, because, as a man of action, thinking always does worry me sorely. You may laugh at what I am going to tell you; if you do I shan’t take offence, because it’s precisely what I should have done had anyone told me about what I’m going to tell you, something that”—the captain hesitated, speaking as if he longed to keep silent; speaking as if afraid of being disbelieved—“something—well, I’ll tell you first that is sounds ridiculous on the face of it, but something which—well, *which I saw myself*.”

“Tell me,” said the cleric, leaning farther forward over the table.

The captain sat up rigid in his chair, took his pipe from between his lips, and spoke as if repeating a lesson that he didn’t understand.

“Once in a Cuban town, in a little Cuban town—can’t remember the precise longitude and latitude—but that’s no matter, and I can’t even remember the name of the town or what I was doing there exactly, but that has no odds on the story.”

“Go on,” said the cleric.

===Chinese===
“多少有点关系，我敢说，”上校慢慢地答道，每一个字都掂过分量，“可是话说回来，也许根本不相干。这两件事我自己是扯不到一块儿去，可我心里总觉得，我本该能把它们扯到一块儿。我试过，使足了劲试，整整一顿早饭都在试，真叫我心焦；因为像我这种干事的人，一动脑子就浑身不得劲。我要讲的这件事，您尽管笑话；您要笑，我也不恼，因为倘若有人先把这桩我要讲的事讲给我听，我也保准要笑话他。有这么一桩事”——上校迟疑起来，那口气像是巴不得闭口不言，又像是生怕人家不信——“有那么一桩事——唔，我先交个底，这事表面上听去荒唐透顶，可它——唔，*是我亲眼所见*。”

“讲给我听，”牧师说着，身子往桌面上又凑近了些。

上校在椅子里坐得笔挺，从嘴里拿下烟斗，说起话来，那腔调像在背一篇自己也不懂的课文。

“有一回，在古巴的一个镇子上，一个古巴小镇——经度纬度是多少，我记不精确了——不过那不要紧；连那镇子的名字我都记不得了，当时在那儿究竟干什么，也说不大上来，可这些都不碍着这段故事。”

“接着讲，”牧师说。

===Original===
“Well, in this little Cuban town I saw an old priest die. He was as dead as this table, you understand; the doctor said so, and I knew it. Well, imagine my horror when half an hour after death this old man arose, entered the next hut, and deliberately, brutally, and carefully stabbed a sleeping child to death.”

The Doctor said nothing, but just looked at the captain.

Jerry stopped eating and looked at Doctor Syn. He was pale, very pale.

Then the captain leaned over the table and continued speaking, but not like a lesson, for there was a thrill in his voice that carried conviction, so Jerry looked at him.

“I found out afterward that the dead fellow had borne a lifelong grudge against his neighbour. The revenge that he had somehow failed to get during his lifetime he accomplished after his death. It was devilish curious.”

===Chinese===
“唉，就在这么一个古巴小镇上，我亲眼看见一位老神甫断了气。他死得同这张桌子一样，是死的——您明白吗？大夫这么说了，我心里也有了数。好了，您想想我该有多骇异吧：人死了半个钟头，这位老人竟爬起身来，走进隔壁一间小屋，存着心、下得狠手而又不慌不忙地，把一个睡着的孩子活活捅死了。”

医生一声不吭，只是望着上校。

杰里停住了嘴，望着辛医生。他的脸白了，白得很。

这时上校把身子探过桌面，继续往下讲，可不再像背课文了，因为他的嗓音里带着一股颤动，叫人不能不信，杰里便又朝他望去。

“后来我打听明白了，那死鬼同他的邻居结的是一辈子的仇。他活着的时候不知怎么总没能报成的仇，死了倒报成了。真是邪门得叫人发毛。”

===Original===
“It was a devilish trick,” explained the Doctor. “The fellow was feigning death to a good purpose—namely, to put his neighbour off his guard. He was not really dead. It would be against all laws of nature—why, of course it would—for a man to arise and walk and commit a foul murder half an hour after his decease! Nonsense, fanciful nonsense!”

“Against the laws of nature, I’ll allow,” went on the captain, as if he had fully expected that his story would be disbelieved, “but if you’ll excuse me saying so, who are you, Doctor Syn, and for the matter of that who am I, to say what the laws of nature are, or to dare to affirm just how far they extend? For my own part, I should prefer to question my own ignorance rather than the laws of nature.”

“But in what way do you hint at a connection between this story and our present trouble in the village owing to this murdering-mad seaman?”

“Why, just this,” went on the captain deliberately. “When you caught sight of this same murdering-mad seaman—you remember, last night, outside the barn—I noticed that you took cold all of a sudden; you got the shivers.”

“Marsh ague—marsh ague,” put in the cleric quickly. “Get it often in this place. Poor old Pepper used to tell me that it was the result of malaria I once had badly in Charleston, Carolina; nearly lost my life with it. Mosquito poisoning which brought on raging malaria. I dare say he was right: I’m a frequent sufferer. As soon as the mists rise from the Marsh I get the shivers.”

===Chinese===
“那不过是恶魔似的花招罢了，”医生解释道，“那家伙是装死，装死是别有用心的——为的就是叫他的邻居放松戒备。他并没有真死。一个人断了气半个钟头之后，还能爬起来走路，去干一桩肮脏的凶杀——这是违背一切自然法则的！呸，当然违背！胡说八道，异想天开的胡说八道！”

“违背自然法则，这我认，”上校接着说，那神气仿佛早料定他讲的故事没人肯信，“可是恕我直言一句：您辛医生算老几，我又算老几，凭什么来断定自然法则是什么，又敢断言这些法则的边界究竟伸到哪里？要我说，我宁可怀疑的是自己的无知，而不是自然法则。”

“可您这话，暗示的又是什么呢？这个故事，同我们村子里眼下因那个杀人杀疯了的水手而起的麻烦，能有什么牵连？”

“就是这个，”上校不慌不忙地往下说，“就在您瞥见那个杀人杀疯了的水手的时候——您记得的，昨天夜里，谷仓外头——我留意到您霎时变了脸色，浑身发冷；您打起寒颤来了。”

“湿地的寒热病——湿地的寒热病，”牧师赶忙插嘴，“这地方的人常犯。可怜的老佩珀（Pepper）从前常对我说，这毛病的根子是一场疟疾，我从前在卡罗来纳的查尔斯顿（Charleston, Carolina）害过一场凶险的疟疾，差一点把命都丢了；是蚊虫的毒引起的恶性疟。我想他说得不错：我是个老病号了。湿地上的雾气一起，我就打寒颤。”

===Original===
“Ah, then there falls one of my points to the ground. Still I have another ready. Suppose we grant that your attack of ague had nothing to do with your sudden meeting with this man.”

“Of course it hadn’t,” muttered the Doctor. “Absurd!”

“Very well, then, did you notice that the entire weight of the rum barrel was carried by Bill Spiker, the gunner?”

“No,” said the Doctor, “I didn’t notice that.”

“No more did Bill Spiker,” said the captain; “you can lay to that, or he would have soon raised objections; but I did notice it, because it’s my business to note which of my men work hardest, you understand; for in cases of preferment I have to give my opinion.”

===Chinese===
“啊，这么一来，我的一条论据就落了空。不过我还备着另一条。姑且算您这场寒热病的发作，同您冷不防撞见此人毫不相干。”

“自然不相干，”医生咕哝道，“荒唐！”

“很好。那么，那只朗姆酒桶的分量，从头到尾都是炮手比尔·斯派克（Bill Spiker）一个人扛的，这一节您可留意到了？”

“没有，”医生说，“我没留意。”

“比尔·斯派克自己也没留意，”上校说，“这个您大可放一百二十个心，不然他早就叫起撞天屈来了；可我留意到了，因为留意我手下哪个人干活最卖力气，正是我的本分，您明白；遇着提拔升迁的事，我总得拿出个意见来。”

===Original===
“I don’t see what that has to do with the case,” said the Doctor. “It’s a common enough complaint to find a man shirking work.”

“Not when the man who shirks is an enthusiastic and willing worker. That’s what made me wonder in the first place, and I’ve now come to the conclusion that whenever the mulatto was ordered to work alone—alone, mind you, without the help of the other seamen—why, he could accomplish anything, but when he was working with anybody, he seemed, in spite of himself, to become singularly useless.”

“You call yourself dense, Captain, and you affirm that I am not; but you seem to have a keener perception of the abstruse and vague than I have, or can even follow.”

“You will be able to follow me in a moment,” said the captain humbly. “I fear it is the poor way in which I am getting to the point; but I have to tell things in my own way, not being given to talk much.”

“Go on, then, in your own way,” said the cleric.

===Chinese===
“我看不出这一节同案子有什么相干，”医生说，“发现有人躲奸偷懒，本是顶平常的事。”

“可要是躲奸的偏偏是个热火朝天、心甘情愿的干活好手呢？起初正是这一点叫我纳闷；如今我得出了结论：只要差那黑白混血儿单独干活——单独，您记着，没有旁的水手帮手——嘿，他什么都办得成；可只要同谁搭了伙，他便身不由己似的，出奇地不中用了。”

“您说自己愚钝，上校，还一口咬定我不愚钝；可依我看，对于这些玄奥含糊的事情，您那点悟性比我灵得多，我连跟都跟不上。”

“一会儿您就跟得上我了，”上校自谦地说，“只怕是我笨嘴拙舌，迟迟说不到点子上；可我讲事情只能用我自己这套讲法，我这人素来不爱多说话。”

“那就照您自己的讲法讲下去，”牧师说。

===Original===
“I then recollected that in my short acquaintance with this mulatto I never remember to have seen him in actual contact with anyone, or anything. And I also recollect a strong tendency among the men to avoid him—in fact, to keep out of any personal contact with him.”

“Natural enough,” explained the cleric. “It is the white man’s antipathy toward a native. Perfectly natural.”

“Perfectly,” agreed Captain Collyer. “And I think we may add the Englishman’s antipathy toward the uncanny and mysterious.”

“I dare say,” said Doctor Syn.

===Chinese===
“后来我又回想起一件事：同这黑白混血儿交往虽然不长，我却不记得曾见他同任何人、任何东西实实在在地碰触过。我还想起，水手们个个都明显地躲着他——说实在的，就是避免同他发生任何身体上的接触。”

“再自然不过了，”牧师解释道，“这是白人对土人的反感。再自然不过。”

“再自然不过，”科利尔上校（Captain Collyer）表示同意，“而且我想还可以添上一条：英国人对怪异神秘之事的反感。”

“也许是，”辛医生说。

===Original===
“I am sure of it,” went on the captain. “Indeed, I went so far as to ask the bo’sun, who has had most dealings with the fellow, whether he had ever touched him.”

“*Touched* him? What do you mean?” asked the parson, who began dimly to see what the other was driving at.

“*Touched, touched* him,” repeated the captain with emphasis. “The bo’sun told me ‘*No*’ and that he wouldn’t care about it, for he considered that ‘a weird-looking cove’—I’ll use his precise way of expressing it—that ‘a weird-looking cove with a face like a dead ’un, what never took food nor drink to his knowledge, weren’t the sort of cove that a respectable seaman wanted to touch.’ ”

Jerry looked at the Doctor. He was as white as the snowy tablecloth before him. Yet he still feigned not to quite follow the captain’s meaning.

“And now,” asked the captain, “mad as it sounds, do you see any connection between the two cases? It’s plain to any traveller or reader of travel books that some of these foreign rascals, especially the priests, possess strange, weird gifts that the white man’s brain runs short of, and I want to know if you see any connection between the two cases.”

===Chinese===
“这个我有数，”上校接着说，“说真的，我还特地去问过水手长（bo’sun）——同这家伙打交道最多的就是他——问他可曾碰过他。”

“*碰过*他？您这是什么意思？”牧师问；他已经隐隐约约看出对方的话是冲着什么来的了。

“*碰过，碰过*他，”上校着重地重复道，“水手长回我说‘*没有*’，还说他也乐得如此，因为在他看来——我用他的原话——‘一个瞧着邪性的家伙，一张脸跟死人似的，据他所知从来不进一口饭、不沾一滴水，那样的家伙，可不是正派水手愿意去碰的。’”

杰里望了望医生。他的脸白得同面前那雪白的桌布一样。可他仍旧装出一副没大听懂上校话音的样子。

“那么如今，”上校问道，“这话听去虽然疯疯癫癫，您可瞧得出这两桩事之间有什么关联？凡是出过门的、读过游记的人都明白：这些外国无赖当中，有的——尤其是那些神甫——身怀一种白人的脑子所欠缺的奇怪邪门的能耐；我想知道的是，您可瞧得出这两桩事之间有什么关联。”

===Original===
Doctor Syn’s hand was trembling, so much so that the long clay pipe stem snapped between his finger and thumb. Neither seemed to notice this, though the lighted ashes had fallen out of the bowl upon the tablecloth and had burned innumerable holes in it before going out.

“Do you see any connection, Doctor Syn?” asked the captain, leaning right over the table and bringing his face close to the cleric.

Doctor Syn did not answer.

The captain repeated the sentence once more—with all the emphasis and force that he could put into his compelling voice:

“Any connection between the Cuban priest who was able to commit deliberate murder after death by controlling the enormous will power of his revenge upon that one definite object? Do you see any connection, I say, between that man and a man who was marooned upon a coral reef in the Southern Pacific being able to follow his murderer across the world in the beastly hulk of his dead self? I don’t understand it, nor do you, perhaps, but I fancy that I see the semblance of a connection, and what I want to know is, can you?”

Then Doctor Syn did a surprising thing: He slowly raised his face to the level of the captain’s, then brought his eyes to meet the captain’s gaze, and then, drawing his lips apart, laying his white teeth bare, he slowly drew over his face, from the very depths of his soul, it seemed, a smile—a fixed smile that steadily beamed all over him for at least a quarter of a minute before he said:

===Chinese===
辛医生的手在发抖，抖得那么厉害，那杆长长的陶土烟斗杆竟在他拇指和食指之间啪地折断了。两个人似乎谁也没留意这个，尽管斗钵里燃着的烟灰已经跌落在桌布上，烫出数不清的窟窿，才慢慢熄掉。

“您瞧得出关联吗，辛医生？”上校问道，把身子整个探过桌面，脸凑得离牧师近近的。

辛医生没有作答。

上校把那句问话又重复了一遍——把他那慑人的嗓音里所能使出的全部分量和力气都使了出来：

“那位古巴神甫，死后犹能把他那股报仇的骇人意志，全数凝在那一个注定的目标上，从而干成一场蓄意的谋杀；而另一边，是一个被抛撇在南太平洋珊瑚礁上的人，竟能拖着他自己那具死尸的骇人躯壳，追着他的仇人走遍天涯——这两个人之间，可有什么关联？我说，您瞧得出其中的关联吗？这道理我弄不懂，或许您也弄不懂，可我自以为瞧见了一丝关联的影子；我想知道的是，您瞧得出吗？”

这时，辛医生做出了一件叫人吃惊的事：他缓缓抬起脸来，同上校的脸平齐，接着把目光迎上上校的逼视，随后分开双唇，露出雪白的牙齿，慢慢地——仿佛是从灵魂的最深处——把一个微笑漫上脸来；那是一个凝定的笑，稳稳地漾遍他的全身，足足有一分钟的四分之一，他才开口说：

===Original===
“You most remarkable man! A King’s captain, eh? I vow you have mistaken your calling.” And he deliberately and with the flat of his white hand patted the captain’s rough cheek, patted it as though the captain were a child being petted or a puppy being teased.

“What the thunder do you mean?” roared the infuriated officer, “by *calling*? Mistake my *calling*?”

“Your profession,” said Doctor Syn, calmly putting on his cloak and hat.

“What would you have me then?” cried the seaman.

“I wouldn’t have you any other than what you are, sir,” replied Doctor Syn, with his hand on the door latch—“a thoroughly entertaining and vastly amusing old seadog, mahogany as a dinner wagon, and loaded with so many fancies as to be creaking near the breaking point.”

The captain was so taken aback with the extraordinary manner of the Doctor that he could only look and gasp. Doctor Syn, perfectly at ease, opened the door.

===Chinese===
“您这个人真是了不得！还是国王的上校呢，嗯？我敢说您是入错了行当。”说着，他从容不迫地抬起白手的手掌，在上校那粗粝的面颊上轻轻拍了拍，拍得就像上校是个受了爱抚的娃娃，或是只遭人逗弄的小狗。

“你这话到底是什么见鬼的意思？”被激怒的军官吼道，“还*行当*？入错我的*行当*？”

“我是说您的职业，”辛医生说着，不慌不忙地披上斗篷，戴上帽子。

“那您要我做个什么呢？”那水手嚷道。

“旁的什么我都不要您做，先生，您现在这样，就顶好，”辛医生答道，一只手已经搭上了门闩——“一条有趣之至、妙趣无穷的老海狗，红木脸色，活像一口送菜的柜车，肚子里装的怪念头又太多，压得咯吱咯吱响，眼看就要散架了。”

医生这一手实在出奇，上校惊得目瞪口呆，只会干瞪着眼喘粗气。辛医生神态自若，拉开了房门。

===Original===
“I wonder?” he said in a low voice, almost tenderly, Jerry thought.

The captain, with a great effort, managed to ejaculate, “What?”

“Why your mother sent you to sea, for as an apothecary—an apothecary—aye, yes, indeed, what a magnificent analyzing apothecary the world has missed in you, sir.” And to the captain’s amazement and Jerry’s astonishment the vicar went out, closing the door behind him.

The captain could do nothing but stare at the closed door, while Jerry, perceiving nothing entertaining in that, stared at the captain, who suddenly exploded out in his great sea voice:

“An apothecary, an analyzing apothecary! What in the devil’s name does he mean by that?”

Jerry still looked at the captain. Certainly he had never beheld anyone more unlike an apothecary. By the widest stretch of his imagination he could not picture the captain mixing drugs or making experiments.

===Chinese===
“我不知道呢？”他低声说，在杰里听来，那语气几乎算得上温柔。

上校好大一阵挣扎，才迸出一个字来：“什么？”

“亏得令堂把您送下了海；不然呀，做个药剂师——药剂师——是啊，不错，真的，先生，这世上可就错过了一位顶了不起的分析药剂师了。”说罢，也不管上校如何惊愕、杰里如何错愕，那位牧师便走了出去，随手带上了门。

上校没别的可做，只有瞪着那扇关上的门；杰里呢，觉得这事儿瞧不出什么乐子来，便瞪着上校；上校忽然用他那洪亮的海嗓子炸开了锅：

“药剂师，分析药剂师！他这话到底是什么见鬼的意思？”

杰里还是望着上校。可不，他从没见过谁比上校更不像药剂师的了。任凭他的想象力天马行空，也描画不出上校配方抓药、摆弄试验的模样。

===Original===
“It’s my opinion—” he said, and then hesitated.

“Yes?” thundered the captain, with an eagerness that seemed to welcome any opinion.

“—well, it’s my opinion, sir, that Doctor Syn is off his head—mad, sir.”

“And it’s my opinion, potboy,” said the captain, as if he valued his own opinion as highly as Jerry Jerk’s, “it’s my opinion that he’s nothing of the kind. He’s feigning madness. He had to do something, you see, to get out of the room, so he called me something that he knew would take my breath away for the moment, knowing me to be dense, and he succeeded, for if any man was unqualified to be an apothecary, I’m the fellow. An analyzing apothecary!”

Then the captain sat down in the armchair and laughed till the tears rolled down his cheeks, and Jerry was obliged to join in, though he didn’t know what he was laughing at. At length he stopped and became most suddenly grave. Getting up, he placed his hands on Jerry’s shoulders.

“Look here, potboy,” he said, “you and I have common secrets that I know. What the devil you were doing out on the Marsh the night before last I don’t know, but that you saw the schoolmaster kill Pepper I *do* know.”

===Chinese===
“依我看——”他说了半句，又迟疑起来。

“讲？”上校如雷地说，那股急切劲儿，倒像随便什么意见都欢迎。

“——唔，依我看，长官，辛医生是疯了头——疯了，长官。”

“那我也有个意见，跑堂小厮，”上校说，那口气仿佛把他自己的意见看得同杰里·杰尔克的意见一般金贵，“依我看，他压根儿不是那路毛病。他是装疯。您想，他总得想个法子脱身出屋，于是便骂了我一句，他知道那句话保管叫我一时喘不上气来；他知道我愚钝，这一招果然灵验，因为要说谁最没资格当药剂师，那就是我这条汉子。分析药剂师！”

说罢，上校便坐进扶手椅里，放声大笑，笑得眼泪顺着两颊直淌；杰里也只好跟着笑，虽然他自己也不知道笑的是什么。到末了，上校收住笑，冷不丁地一本正经起来。他站起身，两手按住杰里的肩膀。

“听着，跑堂小厮，”他说，“你我之间有共同的秘密，这我心里有数。前天夜里你跑到湿地上究竟干什么，我不知道；可你亲眼看见学堂先生杀了佩珀，这个我*确实*知道。”

===Original===
“You know?” cried Jerk, utterly astonished. “Then Doctor Syn must have told you, for I never breathed a word.”

“I know all about it, my boy, because I was hiding in the same dyke as you. Now see here, from what I’ve seen of you, I imagine you can be relied upon. We’ll pluck a leaf out of that parson’s book. We’ll find out his mystery. We’ll find out the whole mystery of this damned Marsh, and as to being apothecaries, why, damme, so we will. We’ll take him at his word.”

“And be apothecaries, sir?” asked Jerry, more puzzled now than ever.

“Yes,” cried the captain, slapping his great hands up and down upon Jerry’s shoulders. “Apothecaries make experiments, don’t they?”

“I dare say they do, sir,” replied Jerk.

“Well, so will we, my lad,” went on the captain, as happy as a sand boy. “We’ll set a trap for all this mystery to walk into. We’ll set a big trap, my lad—big enough to hold all the murderers and mulattoes on the Marsh, the demon riders as well, and certainly not forgetting the coffins in Mipps’s shop nor the bottles of Alsace Lorraine beneath this floor. We’ll catch the lot, my boy, and analyze ’em. Yes, damn ’em! we’ll analyze ’em, inside and outside, by night and by day. And when we’ve analyzed ’em thoroughly, why, we’ll give ’em to Jack Ketch—to old Jack Ketch, who’ll hang ’em up to dry. Not a word, my boy, to anyone; not a word. Here’s a guinea bit to hold your tongue; and look to hear from me before the day’s out, for I shall want your help tomorrow night.”

===Chinese===
“您知道？”杰尔克大叫，惊得目瞪口呆，“那准是辛医生告诉您的，因为我可是一个字都没漏过。”

“这事我全知道，我的孩子，因为我就躲在同您一条沟渠里。好了，听着，照我对你这几面的观察，我想你这人靠得住。咱们就照那位牧师的书上撕下一页来，照样画葫芦。咱们要探出他的秘密。咱们要把这片该死的湿地上的全部秘密，都翻个底朝天。至于当药剂师嘛，嘿，赌个咒，当就当。他既然话说到这份上，咱们就领他这个话头。”

“当药剂师，长官？”杰里问，这下他比先前愈发糊涂了。

“不错，”上校大叫，两只蒲扇般的大手在杰里的肩膀上拍上拍下，“药剂师是要做试验的，不是么？”

“想来是做的，长官。”杰尔克答道。

“好，那咱们也做，我的小子，”上校接着说，快活似神仙。“咱们要下一座套子，让这一整套神神鬼鬼自己走进去。咱们要下一座大套子，我的小子——大得装得下湿地上的全部杀人凶手和黑白混血儿，魔骑也在其内，自然更忘不了米普斯（Mipps）铺子里的那些棺材，还有这地板底下的那几瓶阿尔萨斯-洛林葡萄酒。咱们要把他们一窝端，我的孩子，然后一样样地化验。不错，咒他们的！咱们要把他们里里外外、白天黑夜地化验个透。等化验得一清二楚了，嘿，就把他们交给刽子手杰克·凯奇（Jack Ketch）——交给老杰克·凯奇，由他把他们吊起来晾干。不许走漏半个字，我的孩子，对谁都不许；半个字也不许。这儿是一个几尼金币，买你的嘴严；今天天黑之前，等我的信儿，明天夜里，我要借重你的帮手。”

===Original===
And the captain was gone. Literally rushed out of the door he had, leaving Jerk alone in a whirl.

“Well,” he said to himself, “if a man ever deserved a third breakfast, I’m the one, and here goes; for both of these fellows is stark, staring mad, though it’s wonderful the way they all seems to take to me.”

And thrusting the precious guinea bit into his pocket, Jerk again vigorously attacked the victuals.

===Chinese===
上校就这么走了。说走便当真冲出了房门，撇下杰尔克一个人，脑子里天旋地转。

“得，”他自言自语道，“要说有谁配再吃一顿第三回的早饭，那就是我了，且吃无妨；反正这两位都是疯得彻头彻尾、明明白白的疯子，可他们一个个偏偏都这么待见我，也真叫人啧啧称奇。”

杰尔克把那枚宝贝几尼金币往兜里一揣，又精神抖擞地朝那些吃食大干起来。

## XXIII. A Young Recruit / 二十三　年轻的新伙计

===Original===

“Talk about an ’ealthy child, and there he is,” said Mrs. Waggetts, entering the sanded parlour with Sexton Mipps. “And eat; nothing like eating to increase your fat, is there, Mister Mipps? But, there, I suppose you never had no fat on you to speak of, ’cos if ever a man was one of Pharaoh’s lean kine, you was.”

“It’s hard work wot’s kept me thin, Missus Waggetts,” replied the sinister sexton; “hard work and scheming; and a little of both would do our young Jerry here no harm.”

“As to work,” replied Jerry, gulping down more food, “there ain’t been no complaints against me, I believes, Missus Waggetts?”

“Certainly not, Jerry, my boy,” replied that lady affably.

===Chinese===

“说起结实的娃儿，喏，眼前就有一个，”瓦格茨（Waggetts）太太领着司事米普斯（Mipps）走进那间铺沙的客厅时说道。“还真能吃；要长膘，没有比吃更灵的了，米普斯先生，你说是不是？不过，唉，依我看，你身上那点肥膘压根儿不值一提，因为要说谁像法老梦里那几头瘦牛，那头一个就得数你。”

“叫我瘦下来的是苦活儿，瓦格茨太太，”那个阴恻恻的司事答道；“苦活儿，外加费心盘算；这两样沾上一点，对咱们这儿的杰里小哥，也决没有坏处。”

“说到干活儿嘛，”杰里（Jerry）回道，又往嘴里扒了一大口吃食，“想必还没人抱怨过我偷懒吧，瓦格茨太太？”

“当然没有，杰里，我的好孩子，”那位太太和和气气地答道。

===Original===

“That’s good,” said Jerk, and then turning to the sexton he added: “And as to scheming, Mister Sexton, how do you know I don’t scheme? Some folks are so took up with their own schemes that p’raps they don’t get time to notice wot others are a-doin’. I has lots of schemes, I has. I thinks about ’em by day, I does, and dreams of ’em at night.”

“And they gives you a rare knack of puttin’ away Missus Waggetts’ victuals, I’m a-noticin’,” dryly remarked the sexton.

“Lor’, I’m sure he’s heartily welcome to anything I’ve got,” returned the landlady. “It fair cheers me up to see him eat well, and it’ll be a fine man he’ll be making in a year or so.”

“Aye, that I will,” cried young Jerk; “and when I’m a hangman I ain’t a-goin’ to forget my old friend. I’ll come along from the town every Sunday, I will, and we’ll go and hear Parson Syn preach just the same as we does now, and Mister Mipps will show us into the pew, and everybody will turn round and stare at us and say: ‘Why, there goes Hangman Jerk!’ Then we’ll come back and have a bite of supper together, that is providing I don’t have to sup with the squire at the courthouse.”

===Chinese===

“那就好，”杰尔克说，接着又转向司事，补了一句：“至于盘算嘛，司事先生，您怎么知道我就不会盘算？有的人光顾着自己的盘算，只怕就腾不出工夫来，瞧瞧别人都在干些什么了。我的盘算多着呢，多着呢。白天我琢磨它们，真的，夜里做梦还梦见它们呢。”

“我还瞧出来了，这些盘算叫你得了一手少见的本事——消受瓦格茨太太的吃食，”司事干巴巴地说。

“哎哟，他爱吃我这点东西，我打心眼里欢迎，”老板娘应道。“瞧他吃得这么香，我这心里真叫一个敞亮；再过一年半载，准能出落成一条好汉。”

“没错，我一定出落得成，”小杰尔克嚷道；“等我当上了刽子手，也决不会忘掉老朋友。每个礼拜天我都从城里来，一定的；咱们还照眼下这样，去听辛牧师讲道，米普斯先生引咱们入座，人人都要回过头来，瞪着眼睛瞧咱们，说：‘哎呀，刽子手杰尔克来啦！’然后咱们再回来，一块儿吃口夜宵——当然，前提是我不必在议事厅里陪乡绅用夜宵。”

===Original===

“That ’ud be likely,” interrupted Mipps.

“And, after we’ve had supper, I’ll tell you stories about horrible sights I’ve seen in the week, and terrible things I’ve done, and it’ll go hard with Sexton Mipps to keep even with me with weird yarnin’, I tells you.”

“Ha! ha!” chuckled Mipps. “Strike me dead and knock me up slipshod in a buckrum coffin, if this man Jerry Jerk don’t please me. Look at him, Missus Waggetts. Will you please do me the favour of lookin’ at him hard, though don’t let it put you off your feed, Jerry. Why, at your age I had just such notions as you’ve got, but then I never had your advantages. Why, at thirteen years of age I was as growed up in my fancies as this Jerk. Sweetmeats to devil, eh, Jerry? for it’s some who grows above such garbage from their first rocking in the cradle. This Jerry Jerk is a man; why, bless you, he’s more a man than lots of ’em what thinks they be. Aye, more a man than some of ’em wot’s a-doin’ man’s work.”

“That’s so,” said Mrs. Waggetts, enthusiastically backing the sexton up. “And don’t you forget that he owns a bit of land on the Marsh, and so he’s a Marshman proper.”

===Chinese===

“那可保不齐，”米普斯插了一句。

“还有，吃完夜宵，我就讲给你们听：这礼拜里我见过什么骇人的场面，干过什么可怕的勾当；我告诉你，司事米普斯要想拿怪谈跟我打平手，可着实不易呢。”

“哈！哈！”米普斯咯咯直乐。“要是这个杰里·杰尔克（Jerry Jerk）不称我的心，就叫天雷劈死我，再马马虎虎把我钉进一口粗麻布棺材里完事。你瞧瞧他，瓦格茨太太。劳您的大驾，使劲儿瞧瞧他——杰里，可别让这一瞧倒了你的胃口。嗐，我在你这个岁数，怀的正是你这般念头，只是我从没享过你这样的好条件。是呀，我十三岁的时候，心思意想就跟这杰尔克一样成人了。糖果什么玩意儿，见鬼去吧，是不是，杰里？有的人打摇篮里头一回被摇晃起，就长过了这类垃圾的年纪。这个杰里·杰尔克是条汉子；老天在上，他比许多自以为是汉子的家伙更够汉子。是啊，比有些正干着男子汉营生的家伙，也更够汉子。”

“这话一点不错，”瓦格茨太太热心地替司事帮腔。“你可别忘了，他在湿地上还有一小块地呢，所以他是个地地道道的湿地汉子。”

===Original===

“I doesn’t forget it,” said Mipps, “and I’ve been tellin’ certain folk wot had, how things were goin’ with Hangman Jerk, and I’ve made ’em see that although only a child in regard to age, he ain’t no child in his deeds, and so they agreed with me, Missus Waggetts, that it ’ud be unjust not to let him have full Marshman’s privileges; and I’ll go bail that Jerk won’t disgrace me by not livin’ up to them privileges.”

“P’raps I won’t, Mister Sexton, when I knows what them privileges are.”

“You listen and I’ll tell you,” answered the sexton. “And listen well, Jerry,” added Mrs. Waggetts, “for what Mister Mipps is a-goin’ to say will like as not be the makin’ of you.”

“I will listen most certainly,” replied Jerk, “so soon as Mister Mipps gets on with it. I’m all agog to listen, but there’s no use in listenin’ afore he begins, is there now?”

===Chinese===

“这个我没忘，”米普斯说；“我一向见着那几位说了算的人物，就把刽子手杰尔克的近况报给他们听，让他们明白：论年纪，他固然还是个孩子；论行事，却全然不是孩子了。于是他们也都同意我的看法，瓦格茨太太：不让他享有湿地汉子的全部特权，于理不公。我敢打包票，杰尔克决不会干出配不上那些特权、叫我丢脸的事来。”

“司事先生，等我明白了那特权究竟是什么，兴许我还真就做不到呢。”

“你听着，我就讲给你，”司事答道。“好好听着，杰里，”瓦格茨太太又添了一句，“米普斯先生眼下要说的话，多半就要成全你这个人了。”

“那我一定洗耳恭听，”杰尔克答道，“只要米普斯先生这就开讲。我听都听得心痒难耐了，可他还没开讲，先听个什么劲儿呢，你说是不是？”

===Original===

“Jerry,” said the sexton, “you’re just one after my own heart. You ought to have lived in my days, when I was a lad. Gone to sea and got amongst the interestin’ gentlemen like I did. Aye, they *was* interestin’. And reckless they was, too. They was rough﻿—none rougher; but I don’t grudge ’em all the kicks they give me. Why, it made a man o’ me, young Jerk. I tell you, Master Jerry, that bad as them sea adventurers was, and bad they was﻿—my eye﻿—yes, buccaneers, pirates, and all the rest of it﻿—but bad as they was they did some good, for they made a man o’ me, Jerry. I should never have been the sort o’ man I is now if them ruffians hadn’t kindly knocked the nonsense out o’ me.”

“Shouldn’t you, though?” said Jerry.

“Never, never!” said the sexton with conviction. “But mind you,” he went on, “you has advantages wot I never had. I had to learn all the tricks o’ my trade, and I had to buy my experience. There was no kind friend to teach me my tricks o’ trade, no benevolent old cove wot ’ud pay for my experience. No, I had to buy and learn for myself, but, my stars and garters! afore they’d done with me I had ’em all scared o’ me. Even England hisself didn’t a-relish my tantrums; and when I was in a regular blinder, why, I solemnly believes he was scared froze o’ me. There was only one man my superior in all the time I sailed them golden seas, and that man was Clegg hisself. I served on his ship, you know, Jerk. I was carpenter, master carpenter, mind you, to Clegg hisself﻿—to no less a man than Clegg. And on Clegg’s own ship it were, too. She was called the *Imogene*. I never knew why she was called so. It sounds a high fiddaddley sort o’ name for a pirate ship, but then Clegg was a regular gentleman in his tastes. Why, I remember him sittin’ so peaceful on the roundhouse roof one day a-readin’ of Virgil﻿—and not in the vulgar tongue, neither. He was a-readin’ it in the foreign language wot it was first wrote in, so he told me. And you couldn’t somehow get hold o’ the fact that that benign-lookin’ cove wot was sittin’ there so peaceful a-readin’ learned books had maybe half an hour before strung up a mutineer to the yardarms or made some wealthy fat merchant walk the dirty plank. No, he was a rummun, and no mistake, was that damned old pirate Clegg. But I’d pull my forelock, supposing I had one, all day long to old Clegg, even were I the Archbishop of Canterbury and he only an out-at-heel seadog. Now with England it was different, as I told you, though I’ll own he could beat the devil hisself for blasphemy when he was put out. But I wasn’t afraid o’ him; he was one you could size up like. But Clegg﻿—oh, he was different. Show me the man wot could size up Clegg, and I’d make him Leveller of Romney Marsh, aye, King of England, supposin’ I had the power. There was only one man wot I ever seed wot made Clegg turn a hair, and that was a rascally Cuban priest, but then he had devil powers, he had. Ugh!” And the sexton relapsed into silence. His listeners watched him, and, watching, they saw him shiver. What old scene of horror was flashing before that curious little man’s mind’s eye? Ah, who could tell? No living body, for the crew of the *Imogene* had all died violent deaths one after another in different lands, and since Clegg was hanged at Rye, why, Mipps was the only veteran left of that historical ship of crime, the *Imogene*.

===Chinese===

“杰里，”司事说，“你这孩子呀，正合我这副心肠。你真该活在我年少的那年头，像我一样出海去，混到我当年混的那帮‘有意思的人物’中间。是啊，他们真是有意思，也真是亡命。他们粗野——粗野得没边儿；可他们赏我的那些拳脚，我一点儿也不记恨。嗐，正是那些拳脚把我锤炼成了个男子汉，小杰尔克。我告诉你，杰里少爷，那些海上冒险家坏是真坏——坏透了，我的老天——没错，什么亡命的海盗、真正的匪徒，诸如此类，一应俱全——可再坏，他们也干了件好事：他们把我锤炼成了男子汉，杰里。若不是那帮凶神恶煞发善心敲掉了我满脑袋的荒唐念头，我压根儿成不了如今这号人物。”

“哦？您还真成不了？”杰里说。

“万万成不了，万万成不了！”司事深信不疑地说。“不过你听着……你有的那些好条件，我从前可没有。我这一行的种种门道，全得自己学；我的经验，全得自己掏钱买。没有哪位好心肠的朋友来教我行里的门道，也没有哪位乐善好施的老爷肯替我的经验付账。没有，我只好自己买、自己学；可是，我的老天老爷！等他们把我磨炼完，我已叫他们个个怕我。连英格兰（England）本人都消受不起我的牛脾气；我一闹到性起、大发雷霆，说真的，我怕是把他吓得魂儿都冻住了。我在那片黄金海域上闯荡的那些年，只有一个人在我之上，那人便是克莱格（Clegg）本人。我在他船上当过差，你知道的，杰尔克。我当的是木匠——货真价实的大木匠，你听清楚——侍候的正是克莱格本人——是克莱格那样顶天的大人物。而且上的还是克莱格自己的船。那船叫作‘伊莫金号’（Imogene）。叫这个名字的缘故，我始终没弄明白。做海盗船的名字，听着倒花里胡哨、文绉绉的；不过话说回来，克莱格这个人，论趣味十足是位正牌绅士。嗐，我记得有一天，他安安静静坐在船尾楼顶上读维吉尔（Virgil）——还不是用大白话读的。他是照那部书当初写就的外国文字读的，这是他亲口告诉我的。你简直没法叫自己相信：那个坐在那里安安静静读学问书的慈眉善目的家伙，兴许半个钟头之前，才把一个叛变的水手吊上帆桁，或是逼着哪个脑满肠肥的富商去走那脏兮兮的跳板。不错，那个该死的老海盗克莱格，实实在在是个怪人，半点不假。可我情愿一天到晚给老克莱格扯额发行礼——假如我还有额发可扯的话——哪怕我是坎特伯雷大主教，他不过是一条磨穿鞋跟的老海狗，我也情愿。至于英格兰么，就像我方才说的，那就另是一回事了；虽说我承认，他发起性来，骂起亵渎神明的话，连魔王本人也得甘拜下风。可我不怕他；他是那种你掂量得透的人物。可克莱格——哦，他可不同。谁能掂量得透克莱格，就指给我看；只要我有那个权柄，我就封他做罗姆尼湿地的平定者，甚至做英格兰的王。我平生只见过一个人，叫克莱格心里发过毛，那是个奸诈的古巴神父；不过他有邪魔的本事，他有。呃！”说罢，司事又沉入了沉默。听的人都望着他；望着望着，只见他浑身一哆嗦。是怎样一幕陈年的恐怖光景，正闪过这个古怪小个子人物的心眼？啊，谁说得上来？活人里没有谁能说；因为伊莫金号的船员们，后来在异国他乡一个接一个死于非命；而克莱格在拉伊（Rye）上了绞架之后，那艘罪恶滔天、史上留名的伊莫金号，便只剩下米普斯这最后一个老兵了。

===Original===

“Pray get on with the business in hand, Mister Mipps,” said Mrs. Waggetts, “for though I declare I could a-listen to you a-philosphizin’ and a-moralizin’ all day long, young Jerk is all agog. Ain’t you, Jerry?”

“That’s so,” replied young Jerk. “Please get on, Mister Sexton.”

“I will,” said Mr. Mipps. “You may wonder now, Jerry Jerk, how it has been possible for a swaggerin’ adventurer like I be, or rather was one time, when I was a handsome, fine standin’ young fellow aboard the *Imogene*﻿—I say you may fall to wonderin’ how I come to be a sexton and to live the dull, dreary life of a humdrum villager. Well, I’ll tell you now straight out, man to man, and when I’ve told you, why, you’ll understand all the mystery wot I’m a-gettin’ at.” The sexton smote his hand upon the table so that all the breakfast dishes jumped into different positions on the table, and the two words he said as his fist crashed down were these: “I couldn’t!”

“Couldn’t what?” asked Jerk, whose anxiety for the breakfast dishes’ safety had driven the context of the sexton’s speech from his mind.

===Chinese===

“劳驾，米普斯先生，咱们回到手头的正事上来吧，”瓦格茨太太说；“虽说说真的，你高谈哲理、训世说教，我听上一整天也乐意，可小杰尔克早就心痒难耐了。是不是，杰里？”

“正是，”小杰尔克答道。“请接着讲，司事先生。”

“我会讲下去的，”米普斯先生说。“杰里·杰尔克，你这会儿兴许纳闷：像我这样一个大摇大摆的冒险家——或者说从前的我，当年在‘伊莫金号’上，还是个体面挺拔的英俊小伙——怎么就当上了司事，过起了乡下人单调沉闷的日子。好，如今我干脆直截了当地告诉你，男人对男人地讲；等你听完，我话里绕着的所有玄机，你就全都明白了。”司事一巴掌拍在桌上，震得满桌早餐的杯盘叮叮当当跳着挪了位；拳头砸落的同时，他吐出的两个字是：“办不到！”

“办不到什么？”杰尔克问——他方才光顾着担心那些早餐杯盘的安全，早把司事话头的上下文忘到九霄云外去了。

===Original===

“Couldn’t live a humdrum life after the highjinks I had at sea.”

“But you did, Mister Sexton, and, what’s more, you’re a-doin’ it now,” replied young Jerk with some show of sarcasm.

“And very prettily you can act, can’t you, Hangman Jerk?” said Mr. Mipps, winking. “I declare you’re a past-master in the way of pretendin’. Well, pretendin’ all’s very well, but it’s often plainspoken truth wot serves as a safer weapon for roguish fellows, and it’s plainspoken truth I’m a-goin’ to use to you, believin’ in my heart that if ever there was a roguish fellow livin’, and one after my old heart, why, Hangman Jerk is that fellow.”

“Please get on, Mister Sexton,” said Jerry, feeling rather important.

===Chinese===

“在海上那样大闹过一场之后，办不到再过那种单调乏味的日子。”

“可您不是过上了么，司事先生，不光学上了，眼下还正过着呢，”小杰尔克回道，话里带着几分挖苦。

“你这份装模作样的功夫，可真不赖，是不是，刽子手杰尔克？”米普斯先生眨眨眼睛说。“我说你这假戏真做的本事，真真是个中老手了。不过，装假固然很好，可对无赖汉来说，直来直去的实话往往才是件更保险的家伙什；这会儿，我就要拿直来直去的实话对你说了——我心里认定，要是天底下还活着一个无赖汉，一个正对我这老家伙脾胃的无赖汉，那么，刽子手杰尔克就是此人。”

“请接着讲，司事先生，”杰里说，自觉颇为重要似的。

===Original===

“Yes, get on, get on,” repeated Mrs. Waggetts, “for I’m a-longin’ to hear how he takes it.”

“Can you doubt? I don’t,” replied Mipps. “I bet my head he’ll take it as a man, won’t you, Jerry Jerk, eh?”

“I’ll tell you when I knows wot it is,” replied the boy.

“Why, what a talky old party I’ve become. Time was when I never uttered a word﻿—but do﻿—ah, I was one to do. And much and quick I did, too.”

===Chinese===

“对，讲啊，讲啊，”瓦格茨太太跟着催道，“我正眼巴巴要听他怎么接这话呢。”

“这还用得着疑心？我就不疑心，”米普斯答道。“我拿我这颗脑袋打赌，他准像个男子汉似的接下来。是不是，杰里·杰尔克，啊？”

“等我弄明白那是什么事，我再答复你，”孩子答道。

“瞧瞧，我竟变成了个话痨老头儿。想当年，我一个字也不多说——只管做——啊，我是个做实事的。而且做得又多，又快。”

===Original===

“We knows that very well, thank you, Mister Sexton,” said Jerry. “That is, we knows it if we knows your word can be relied upon.”

“You may lay to that,” said Mipps, “and you may lay that in our future dealings together you can depend on me a-standin’ by you as long as you lay the straight course with me.”

“I’ll take your word for that,” responded Jerk. “Now p’raps you will get on?”

“Well,” said the sexton, “I must begin with the Marsh﻿—the Romney Marsh. No one knows better than you do that she’s a queer sort of a corner, is Romney Marsh. I’ve seen you a-prowlin’ and a-nosin’ about on her. You scented excitement, you did, on the Marsh. You smelt out a mystery, and like a lad of adventurous spirit you wanted to find out the meanin’ of it all. Very natural. I should have done the same when I was a lad. Well, now the whole business is this: the Marsh don’t approve of folks a-nosin’ and a-prowlin’ after her secrets, see?” And the sexton’s face grew suddenly fierce: all those lines of quizzical humour vanished from around that peculiar mouth and left a face of diabolical cruelty, of cunning, and of deceit. But Jerk was not easily unnerved or put out of countenance. There was something about Mipps that put him on his mettle and stimulated him. He liked Mipps, but he liked to keep even with him, for his own self-respect, which was very great, for in some things Jerry Jerk was most inordinately proud.

===Chinese===

“这一点我们清楚得很，多谢，司事先生，”杰里说。“就是说，前提是我们知道您的保证靠得住。”

“这你尽管放一百二十个心，”米普斯说，“你还可以放心的另一样是：往后咱们合伙打交道，只要你跟我行的是一条直航道，我就一准儿站在你这一边。”

“这话我领了，”杰尔克应道。“那么，现在总可以接着讲了吧？”

“好，”司事说，“我得从湿地讲起——罗姆尼湿地（Romney Marsh）。没有谁比你更清楚：罗姆尼湿地是个多古怪的角落。你在这片湿地上四处转悠、东嗅西闻，我全看在眼里。你在湿地上嗅出了刺激，这可是真的；你闻出了一桩神秘事，便凭着少年人的冒险心气，一心要探出个中究竟。再自然不过了。换了我年少那会儿，也一样。好，如今整桩事情的关窍就在这里：湿地可不喜欢有人跟在她背后东嗅西探、打她秘密的主意，明白吗？”说到这里，司事的脸陡然凶相毕露：那张特别的嘴巴四周，那些诙谐逗趣的纹路一扫而空，只剩下满脸恶魔般的残忍、狡诈与虚伪。可杰尔克并不容易被吓住，也不轻易失色。米普斯身上有股劲儿，反倒叫他摩拳擦掌、跃跃欲试。他喜欢米普斯，却也要同米普斯打个平手、不肯落于下风——为了他的自尊心；他的自尊心极重，因为在某些事情上，杰里·杰尔克傲气得很，傲得没有边。

===Original===

“Oh, the Marsh don’t approve, eh? And who or what might be the power on the Marsh to tell you so?”

“The great ruler o’ the Marsh﻿—the man with no name who successfully runs his schemes and makes his sons prosperous.”

“That’ll be the squire, then,” said Jerry promptly, “for he’s the Leveller of Marsh Scotts, ain’t he? He makes the laws for the Marshmen, don’t he?”

“He does that certainly,” agreed the sexton. “But whether or no he’s the power what brings luck to the Marshmen﻿—Marshmen, mind you, worthy of the name﻿—neither you nor me nor nobody can tell. Sufficient for us that the Marsh is ruled by a power, a mysterious power, wot brings gold and to spare to the Marshmen’s pockets.”

===Chinese===

“哦，湿地不喜欢了，是吗？那么湿地上的权柄，又是何人何物，会这样跟您说呢？”

“湿地至高的主宰——那位没有名字的人物：种种大计经他的手无不成，他的儿郎们个个兴旺发达。”

“那想必就是乡绅了，”杰里应得飞快，“他是湿地斯科特人的平定者（Leveller of Marsh Scotts），对吧？湿地人的法律都是他立的，对吧？”

“这个自然，”司事附和道。“可他究竟是不是给湿地汉子带来好运的那股势力——湿地汉子，你听清楚，得是名副其实的湿地汉子——不管你、我，还是随便哪个，都说不准。够咱们受用的只有一条：这片湿地由一股势力管着，一股神秘的势力，它把金子大把大把地送进湿地汉子的腰包，还绰绰有余。”

===Original===

“Ah, then,” said Jerry, with his eyes blazing, “then I was right. There are smugglers on the Marsh.”

“There are,” said the sexton; “and it’s wealthy men they be, though you’d never guess at it, and darin’, adventurous cusses they be, and rollickin’ good times they gets, and no danger to speak of, ’cos the whole blessed concern is run by a master brain wot never seems to make mistakes, and it was this same master brain wot agreed that you should share the privileges o’ the Marsh, and I was ordered to recruit you.”

“Oh! and what’ll be required o’ me?” asked Jerk, “supposin’ I thinks about it.”

“You’ll be given a horse, and you’ll ride with the Marsh witches, learn their trade, and be apprenticed to their callin’.”

===Chinese===

“啊，这么说，”杰里两眼放光，“我猜对了。湿地上当真有走私贩子。”

“有的，”司事道；“而且个个家底殷实，虽说你绝计猜不出来；个个胆大包天、爱闯爱冒险，日子过得撒欢儿一般快活，又谈不上有什么危险；因为这门大买卖从头到尾由一颗高明的头脑掌着舵，那颗脑袋像是从来不出岔子。也正是这同一颗高明的头脑点了头，让你来分享湿地的特权，我这才奉命来招募你。”

“哦！那要我出什么力呢？”杰尔克问，“假使我要想一想的话。”

“会有一匹马交到你手上，你跟着湿地女巫们一道骑行，学她们那门营生，拜在她们那一行的门下当学徒。”

===Original===

“And how do you know I won’t blab and get you and your fellows the rope?” asked Jerry bravely.

“Because we’ve sized you up, we ’as, and we don’t suspect you of treachery. If we did, it wouldn’t much matter to us, though I should be right sorry to have been disappointed in you, for I declare I don’t know when I took to a young man like I ’as to you. You’re my fancy, you are, Jerry. Just like I was at your age. Mad for adventure and for the life of real men.”

“Yes, but just supposin’ that I did disappoint you, Mister Sexton? It’s well to hear all sides, you know.”

“Aye, it’s well and wise, too, and I’ll tell you. If it was to your advantage to betray us﻿—to that captain p’raps﻿—well, I daresay you’d do it now, wouldn’t you?”

===Chinese===

“您又怎么知道，我不会多嘴多舌，把您和您那伙人统统送上绞架呢？”杰里放胆问道。

“因为我们掂量过你这个人，掂量过了，我们不疑心你会背叛。就算疑心，对我们也没什么大碍；只是我要是在你身上看走了眼、失了望，那可真要难过得慌，因为我敢说，我记不得几时曾像对你这样称心过哪个年轻人。你就是我的心头好，杰里，就是。跟我当年在你这个岁数的时候一模一样：冒险入了迷，一心想过真正男子汉的日子。”

“是啊，可万一我果真让您失望了呢，司事先生？把各方的话都听一听，总没有坏处，您知道。”

“不错，这不仅是好事，也算得明智，我就告诉你。倘若出卖我们于你有利——兴许是出卖给那位上校——依我说，这会儿你未必不会干，是不是？”

===Original===

“I don’t know,” said Jerk; “all depends. P’raps I might, though. You never knows, does you?”

“No, you never knows. Quite right. But you’d know one thing: that go where you would, or hide where you liked, we’d get you in time, and when we did get you it ’ud be short shrift for you﻿—you may lay to that.”

“I daresay,” said Jerry, “unless, of course, I got you first.”

“You’d have a good number to get, my lad,” laughed the sexton. “But it’s no use a-harguin’ like this. You won’t betray us when it don’t serve your turn to do so, and it won’t do that, ’cos we has very fine prospects open for you, and advantages. Why, we can set you in the way of rollin’ in a coach before we’ve done with you, and who knows, years hence, when you’re older than you be now, who knows but what you might not succeed to the headship. If anything was to happen to the great chief wot’s to prevent you from takin’ his place, eh? You’re smart, ain’t you? There’s no gainsayin’ that, now, is there, Missus Waggetts?”

===Chinese===

“我不知道，”杰尔克说；“那得看情况。不过兴许我真会干。谁说得准呢，是不是？”

“是啊，谁也说不准。说得对。可有一样你会知道：任你逃到天涯海角，任你藏得再好，早晚我们会逮住你；逮住你的那一天，你就只有速死的份儿了——这你尽管信得真真的。”

“那倒是，”杰里说，“当然啦，除非我先逮住了你们。”

“你要逮的，可不止三五个，我的孩子，”司事笑道。“不过这么抬杠没什么意思。只要出卖我们于你无利，你就不会干；而这件事恰恰于你无利，因为我们为你敞开的可是远大前程，有的是便宜。嗐，等我们把你调教出来，就能让你出门有四轮马车可坐、养尊处优；再说，谁知道呢，过些年头，等你比眼下大了些，说不定你还能接掌大权呢。倘若那位大头领有个万一，又有什么能拦着你去坐他的位子，啊？你机灵，对不对？这一点没得说的，是不是，瓦格茨太太？”

===Original===

“No, indeed,” replied that lady.

“Then take my tip, the straight tip of an old gentleman o’ fortune, and you join us.”

“What’ll I have to do and what is it I’m a-joinin’, though?” asked the boy.

“The great scheme of wool-runnin’,” said Mr. Mipps.

===Chinese===

“是没得说的，”那位太太答道。

“那就听我一句忠告——听一个老冒险家掏心窝子的忠告——入伙吧。”

“那我得干些什么？我入伙的又究竟是什么营生？”孩子问。

“贩羊毛的大计，”米普斯先生说。

===Original===

“Ah,” sighed Jerry, “I thought as much. And what am I to do, always supposin’ that I’m willin’ to join?”

“We’ve a vacancy in the horsemen﻿—a man short, you see, though we’ve got the horse. It’s Mr. Rash’s horse, but we’ve turned out the schoolmaster and kept his horse. He weren’t one of us, you see, so we found that we didn’t want him no more.”

“You’ve killed him?” cried the hangman, starting up.

“I didn’t say that,” retorted the sexton. “I merely remarked that we didn’t want him no more. And now just give me your attention. I’ve every reason to believe, and so has the great chief that I work for, that you are gettin’ very thick with that swab of a King’s captain. Well, now, don’t go suddenly a-givin’ him the cold shoulder, do you see? You can’t drop a friend all at once like a hot potato without excitin’ the gossip and suspicion of folk; so remember what I says and keep civil to him. But it’s my opinion that after tonight you’ll know which side you be on, for once get the thrill of the demon ride and you’ll not want to get dismissed. Besides, gettin’ dismissed by our chief ain’t exactly what you might term a pleasant form of bein’ entertained.”

===Chinese===

“啊，”杰里叹了口气，“我猜也是这个。那我该干些什么呢——当然是假定我愿意入伙。”

“骑兵一队里正有个空缺——你瞧，少一个人手，马倒是现成的。那是拉什（Rash）先生的马；我们把那位学堂先生请了出去，马留了下来。他不是我们的人，你明白，所以我们发觉再也用不着他了。”

“你们把他杀了？”那未来刽子手嚷道，霍地站起身来。

“我可没这么说，”司事顶了回去。“我只是说，我们再也用不着他了。好了，如今竖起耳朵听我说。我有充分的理由相信——我为之效力的那位大头领也一样——你正跟国王手下那位上校混得火热，就是那号饭桶。听着，眼下可千万别突然给他冷脸看，明白吗？朋友这东西，总不能像扔烫手山芋似的，一下子甩掉，不惹得人家说闲话、起疑心；所以记住我的话，对他照旧客客气气。不过依我看，过了今晚，你就知道该站在哪一边了；魔骑行那阵刺激，只消尝过一回，你便舍不得被开革了。再说，叫我们头儿开了革，那滋味恐怕算不上什么好受的款待。”

===Original===

“And what do I do, Mister Sexton?”

“You’ll get told all in good time.”

“But what do the demon riders do?” persisted the boy.

“Frighten folk from the Marsh when the ponies are trottin’ under the wool packs.”

===Chinese===

“那我干什么呢，司事先生？”

“到时候自会样样告诉你。”

“可那些魔骑手干的究竟是什么营生？”孩子追问道。

“趁驮着羊毛包的矮马得得赶路的时候，把湿地上撞见的人吓散。”

===Original===

“And where do the wool packs come from?”

“From nearly every farm on the Marsh.”

“And they put it all in packs and send ’em down to the coast?”

“That’s the ticket, my lad. Pack ’em all up on ponies and bring back coffins full of spirit from France.”

===Chinese===

“羊毛包又是打哪儿来的？”

“湿地上差不多每个农庄都有一份。”

“他们把羊毛统统装成包，运下海边去？”

“正是这么回事，我的孩子。统统装上矮马驮走，再从法国驮回一棺材一棺材的烧酒。”

===Original===

“Coffins full of spirit from France?” repeated the amazed boy.

“Yes, that’s why I’m a coffin-maker. What would you expect to see inside a nailed-up coffin, eh?”

“Why, a dead ’un,” said the boy.

“Exactly; and as folk ain’t particular fond of amusin’ themselves with a sight of dead ’uns they lets my coffins alone, do you see, and the spirit is treated with every respect and is allowed to go on its way very snug and all knocked up most particular solid.”

===Chinese===

“一棺材一棺材的烧酒，打法国运回来？”那孩子惊得重复了一遍。

“可不是，所以我才是棺材匠嘛。一口钉死了的棺材，你指望里头是什么？”

“还能是什么，死人呗，”孩子说。

“正是；人们本就不怎么爱拿死人逗闷子，自然躲着我的棺材走，你明白吧；于是烧酒一路受着十二分的恭敬，舒舒坦坦地赶它的路，棺材还敲得格外结实。”

===Original===

“And the head of it all’s the squire, is it?”

“I never said so,” replied the sexton quickly; “but the less you think and say on that subject the better, for those who know the identity of the great chief would sooner have their eyes put out than betray him; so don’t you hamper your young career with thinkin’ about it. All you’ve got to do is to obey.”

“And what do I get out of it?”

“Gold and the time of your life.”

===Chinese===

“这一切的头儿，就是那位乡绅，对不对？”

“这话我可从来没说过，”司事赶忙答道；“这个题目，你想得越少、说得越少越好；因为凡知道大头领是谁的人，宁可自己戳瞎双眼，也不肯出卖他；所以别拿这种念头去绊你年轻的前程。你只消记牢一件事：服从。”

“那我能从中得着什么？”

“金子，还有一辈子最快活的好日子。”

===Original===

“And when do I start?”

“Tonight.”

“Tonight?” faltered Jerk much relieved, for he had thought of his promise to help the captain, and was greatly thankful that the dates had not clashed.

“At half-past twelve at Old Tree Cottage; but don’t go to the coffin-shop side. Tap at the back kitchen window.”

===Chinese===

“那我几时动身？”

“就在今晚。”

“今晚？”杰尔克声音发颤地问——心里却大大松了口气：他原惦记着自己答应过要帮上校的那件事，正暗自庆幸两个日子没有撞在一处。

“十二点半，老树农舍（Old Tree Cottage）；可别走棺材铺那一面。敲后灶间的窗户。”

===Original===

“And half-past twelve, you say?”

“That’s the time,” answered Mipps, holding out his hands and seizing Jerk’s in both his. “And I can tell at a glance that your a-goin’ to be a credit to the undertakin’.”

And a minute afterward he was gone and Jerk was sent by Mrs. Waggetts into the bar to polish up the tankards.

===Chinese===

“您说，十二点半？”

“就是这个时候，”米普斯答道，一面伸出双手，把杰尔克的手抓在自己两手当中。“我一眼就瞧得出来，往后这桩‘大事’，你准办得风风光光——要知道，我们这一行办的可正是丧家的大事。”

一分钟之后，他人已无影无踪；瓦格茨太太打发杰尔克进酒吧间，把那些大酒杯擦得锃亮。

## XXIV. The Coffin-Maker Has a Visitor / 二十四　棺材匠来了位客人

===Original===
About noon of the same day Captain Collyer, in walking through the village, found himself passing Old Tree Cottage, the low-lying residence of Sexton Mipps, with its coffin shop facing the street and its small farmhouse behind. Attracted by a great noise of hammering, the captain stepped up to the window and glanced in. Rows of coffins lined the walls and coffin planks were everywhere propped up against shelves containing everything imaginable. In the centre of the shop stood two black trestle-stools, and upon these funeral relics reposed a large coffin with no lid. Inside this gloomy thing sat Mr. Mipps. He was sitting straight up and hammering lustily upon the coffin sides, singing away with much spirit to the rhythm:

“O hammer, hammer, hammer,
					

					And damn her, damn her, damn her,
					

					For I don’t fear my wife now she’s dead.”

===Chinese===
同一天晌午，科利尔上校（Captain Collyer）在村里信步走着，不觉已经过了老树农舍（Old Tree Cottage）——那是司事米普斯（Mipps）的低矮住处，棺材铺临街开门，背后还带一座小小的农舍。一阵乒乒乓乓敲打得震天响的动静引得上校起了兴致，他凑到窗前，朝屋里张望。四壁排着一口口棺材，到处都是棺材板，斜倚在架子上，架子里的物件五花八门，应有尽有。铺子正中立着两只黑漆支架凳，就在这两件丧仪什物上，端端正正卧着一口没上盖的大棺材。这口阴森森的物件里头，坐着的不是别人，正是米普斯先生。他直挺挺坐得笔正，抡起锤子往棺材帮上敲得起劲，一面踏着锤点的节奏，精神抖擞地唱：

“哦，敲啊，敲啊，敲啊，
					

					咒她呀，咒她呀，咒她呀，
					

					如今老婆死了，我再也不怕。”

===Original===
The captain, amused at the crude words, pushed open the casement and leaned into the room. Whether the sexton saw him or not the captain did not know, but the song changed immediately to a song of the sea:

“There’s no swab like the captain,
					

					There’s no swab like the captain,
					

					Of all the swabs I’ve ever seen
					

					With a diddle diddle diddle diddle diddle diddle dee
					

					No swab like the captain.”

===Chinese===
上校听得这路粗野的词儿，觉得有趣，便推开窗扇，把身子探进屋里。司事瞧没瞧见他，上校并不知道，只听那支歌立时换了腔，改成了一支海上的歌：

“天底下没有蠢货比得上上校，
					

					天底下没有蠢货比得上上校，
					

					我见过的蠢货数也数不清，
					

					嘀嗒嘀嗒嘀嗒嘀嗒嘀嗒——嘀，
					

					没有蠢货赛得上上校。”

===Original===
“A very appropriate song, Master Sexton,” laughed the captain.

Mipps turned round and surveyed the intruder.

“Why, knock me up solid if it ain’t the good captain! The gold of the high noon to you, sir, though there ain’t much gold in the sky today. I take it as a very friendly piece of impertinence that you should come and look me up so unexpected. Had I knowed of your arrival I’d have had these grisly relics stowed away, for some folk has a distinct dislike to lookin’ at these last dwellin’ houses.”

===Chinese===
“一支再贴切不过的歌，司事老爷，”上校大笑道。

米普斯转过身来，把这位不速之客上下打量。

“嗬，若不是这位好上校大驾光临，就把我结结实实敲打成材！愿正午的金光罩着您，长官——虽说今天这天上的金光可不多。您这般出其不意地登门瞧我，我把这份冒昧当成交情收下了。早知道您要来，我准会先把这堆瘆人的什物归置开，因为有些人瞧见这些‘最后的宅子’，心里老大不痛快。”

===Original===
“You are used to ’em, I suppose, by now?” said the captain.

“Oh, love you, yes, I don’t mind ’em. Some undertakers has fearful superstitions about coffins. Some won’t get in ’em to measure ’em. Lord! I always does. I lies down inside ’em and pops the lid on the top to see if it’s airtight.”

“Awkward if the lid was to stick.”

===Chinese===
“这路玩意儿，您如今想必早就看惯了吧？”上校说。

“哦，老天爷，可不，我才不嫌乎它们。有些殡葬承办人对棺材迷信得邪乎。有的自己死活不肯躺进去量尺寸。老天！我可回回都躺。我往里头一躺，把盖子啪嗒往上一扣，试试严不严实、漏不漏气。”

“盖子要是扣死了卡住，可就尴尬了。”

===Original===
“You may well say that, ’cos once it did. But it weren’t so much awkward as peaceful, for after I’d pushed and struggled for a power o’ time, I just resigned myself to my fate, feelin’ thankful that at any rate I had had the privilege of bein’ my own undertaker. I shall never forget my feelin’s when my last bit of breath came up and went out. It was just the sort o’ feelin’ you gets when you drowns, only more so. ’Cos when you drowns you sees all the bad actions of your life a-troopin’ before you, but gettin’ buried alive is different, ’cos you sees all the good actions wot you’ve done. Mind you, things I’d clean forgot. Little acts of kindness wot I thought could never have been recorded anywhere. Why, they all walked out, and I seemed to be greatly comforted, ’cos, you see, I thought as how I was quite in the runnin’ for heaven. In fact I was so pleased with my past self that I fairly kicked with delight, and that was the means of bringin’ me back to earth, ’cos over went these trestles, and the jar I got knocked the stuck lid off. No, I’ve been near gone these many times, but never so near gone as that, for, as you see, I was finished with the undertaker having undertook myself, and I only had to be passed through the parson’s hands and get knocked over the sconce with the sexton’s shovel, as Shakespeare says in the play, to be a real ‘gonner,’ stiff and proper.”

“A horrible experience, Master Sexton,” returned the captain.

“It was in a sense. But I could tell you horribler. I takes a pride in my business, same as you might in yours. That’s why I went round the world.”

===Chinese===
“您算说着了，只因有一回真给卡住了。不过那滋味与其说是尴尬，倒不如说是安宁：我又推又挣了好大的工夫，末了索性听天由命，心里还谢天谢地，想着好歹享了这份体面，给自己当了一回殡葬承办人。断气的当儿，最后那丝气儿悠悠地冒上来又散出去，那滋味我这辈子忘不了。跟溺水的滋味一模一样，只是更甚。因为溺水的时候，你看见的是一辈子干下的坏事，成群结队打眼前过；活埋可不一样，活埋的时候，你看见的是你这辈子行过的好事。您听听，连我自己都忘得一干二净的事，全都在里头：些小小不言的善举，我原想着哪儿也不会有人记上一笔的。嗐，它们排着队全走了出来，我便觉着大大地得了安慰，因为您瞧，我寻思着自己进天国的指望着实不小。说实在的，我对从前的那个自己满意得紧，欢喜得两只脚直蹬——就这一蹬，倒把我蹬回了阳世：只听支架凳哗啦翻倒，那一震，把卡死的棺材盖也震脱了。不瞒您说，我这条命险险乎交出去过许多回，可从没有像那回那么险，因为您瞧，给自己当殡葬承办人这一节已然办完，往后再经牧师的手一送，再挨上司事的一铲子，敲在脑壳上——正如莎士比亚（Shakespeare）在戏文里说的——我便是个十足十的‘完结之人’了，直挺挺，又体面。”

“好一场骇人的经历，司事老爷，”上校回道。

“某种意义上算是。可我还能讲出更骇人的来。我这行当，我自己得意，就跟您得意您自己那一行是一个理。就为这个，我才周游了世界。”

===Original===
“Oh, you’ve been round the world, have you?” said the captain.

“Not once nor twice, but many times, and do you know why?”

“Perhaps the life of the get-rich-quick buccaneers appealed to you?” remarked Captain Collyer casually.

===Chinese===
“哦，您还周游过世界哪？”上校说。

“岂止一回两回，回数多着呢。您可知道为什么？”

“莫不是那伙儿一夜暴富的海盗营生，正对了您的胃口？”科利尔上校若无其事地问。

===Original===
“There you go—suspicious. Can’t you adapt yourself for five minutes? Can’t you make an effort when you’re a-gossipin’ with honest folk to forget that there is dishonest ones? I never did see the like. Here we be chattin’ quite friendly, and forgettin’ our little differences, when you starts accusin’ me of bein’ a Captain Clegg or an England. Do I look like a bold pirate now? Lookin’ at me straight sittin’ up in this ’ere coffin, could you say that I looked like a swaggerin’ gentleman o’ fortune. No, you couldn’t. Very well, then, why go and make unpleasant insinuations against a respectable sexton o’ the realm? Mind you, I don’t say as how I didn’t come across some of that breed durin’ my travels, and I don’t say as how circumstance, that fickle woman, didn’t at times make me work for ’em. But not for long. I held no sort o’ likes with the likes o’ them, and though some of ’em had most engagin’ ways, it was easy to see that they was all of ’em unadulterated sinners. And swear? God bless your eyes, Captain, it made you blush like a damned woman to hear ’em.”

“And if it was not for gold and adventure that you went, may I ask what tempted you abroad?”

===Chinese===
“您这又来了——疑神疑鬼。您就不能将就着消停五分钟？跟本本分分的人闲话家常的时候，就不能费点神，把那些不安分的忘到脑后去？您这样的人我可真没见过。咱们这儿正聊得亲亲热热，连彼此那点小过节都撂开了，您倒好，张口就指控我是个克莱格船长，要不就是个英格兰。您瞧瞧我这会儿，像个胆大包天的海盗吗？我端端正正坐在这口棺材里，您直瞪瞪地望着，能说我像个神气活现的福运绅士吗？不能，您说不出。很好，那么，何苦跟一个王国治下体体面面的司事过不去，净搁些叫人牙碜的影射？您听着，我可不是说，闯荡四方时没撞见过那号人物；也不是说，‘时运’那个朝三暮四的婆娘，没几回逼着我替他们做过工。只是都做不长。我跟他们那路人合不来，虽说他们里头有的做派倒也招人喜欢，可一眼便看得出，他们个个都是如假包换的罪人。要说骂人？老天爷保佑您这双眼吧，上校，听他们骂上一场，臊得您跟个遭天谴的婆娘似的满脸通红。”

“那敢问一句，既然您出海不是为了金银和冒险，又是什么勾着您去了外洋呢？”

===Original===
“Certainly, Captain. It was the love of my work. The zeal to have a look at other sextons, vergers, and undertakers and see what they were a-doin’ with the business. But Lord love you, Captain, I soon found as how funerals was done on different plans abroad. Why, I could tell you some things I seed with regard to burials abroad what ’ud make your flesh creep—aye, and now, too, though the sun is high in the heaven.”

“Well, I’ve an hour to spare, Master Sexton. What do you say to coming along to the Ship and enjoying a drink and a friendly pipe?”

===Chinese===
“这就告诉您，上校。为的是我疼我这行当。一心想开开眼，瞧瞧别处的司事、领堂员（verger）和殡葬承办人，看他们都是怎么办这桩买卖的。可是老天疼您，上校，我很快便发现，外国的丧事另有一套章程。嗐，外国办下葬的种种光景，我亲眼见过一些，讲出来能叫您汗毛倒竖——就是这会儿也照样叫您汗毛倒竖，虽说日头正高高地挂在天上。”

“这样吧，我正好闲着一个钟头，司事老爷。不如一道上‘船’酒馆去，喝上一盅，亲亲热热抽袋烟，您意下如何？”

===Original===
“I thinks I can do one better than that, thankin’ you kindly,” said the sexton, vaulting with marvellous dexterity out of the lofty coffin to the floor, “for I’ve baccy, pipes, and good brandy all to hand, and if you’d care to spend an hour with Sexton Mipps and listen to his babbles, why, light your ‘strike me dead’ and gulp your spirits and settle your hulk in that there coffin, what hasn’t got no passenger inside—so don’t be frightened—and we’ll shut the window, for it’s a-blowin’ the fire out; and if you ain’t cozy, well, it’s not the fault of the sexton, is it now?” And then Mr. Mipps, after busily providing his guest with the requisites for smoke and drink, and after splitting up a coffin plank to renew the fire, sprang back into the coffin, sitting snug with a glass of brandy and his clay pipe. The captain also was ensconced on a coffin in the corner, and to the crackle of the split coffin plank upon the fire the sexton began to yarn.

===Chinese===
“多谢您好意，不过我想着还能更进一竿，”司事说着，身手矫捷得出奇，一纵身从那口高高的棺材里跳到地上，“烟草、烟斗、上好的白兰地，我这儿全是现成的。您若肯赏光，陪司事米普斯消磨上一个钟头，听听他胡咧咧，那就点上您那杆‘天打雷劈’的烟斗，灌下您的酒，把您那副大身板安顿进那口棺材里去——里头没载着什么客人，不必害怕——咱们再把窗户关上，风正灌进来，把火往灭里吹呢；若是这样还不算舒坦，嘿，那可就不是司事的不是了，您说是不是这个理？”说罢，米普斯先生忙忙碌碌，把抽烟喝酒的种种家什一一供到客人手边，又劈了几块棺材板把火拢旺，随即一个纵身跳回棺材里，就着一杯白兰地、捏着那管陶土烟斗，舒舒服服坐定了。上校也在墙角一口棺材上安顿下来。棺材板在火里烧得噼啪作响，司事便拉开了话匣子。

## XXV. The Sexton Speaks / 二十五　司事开讲

===Original===
“Funerals may be divided into three classes, for there be solemn funerals, there be grisly funerals, and there be funny ’uns. The funniest funeral I ever did see was in China. Do you know, Captain, they very seldom buries out there? They leaves the blasted coffins above ground. The whole of the countryside is a-littered with ’em. For untidy burials China waves the flag, and they has other very funny customs about funerals out there, too. When a fellow goes and dies out there it’s a devil of a business he has to go through before he gets fixed up final. Every family out there ’as their own very particular priest, you understand, and this very particular priest is always a very sly sort o’ dog. The dead ’un is put into the coffin, and then the family pays their sly dog a considerable sum o’ money in exchange for very hard prayers wot the sly dog makes for ’em to his gods. He goes away and prays for weeks on end, askin’ his gods just where exactly the family ought to bury their dead ’un to enable him to get into heaven by the most convenient route. And as the sly dog gets paid all the time he’s a-prayin’, you can bet your wig that he pretends to string them prayers out to some length. And I can tell you those Chinese parsons were up to one or two smart wrinkles. I’ll tell you about a certain Ling Fu Quong. Well, if I hadn’t rung the curtain down, as the stage players say, upon that gent’s little comedy, I believes he’d be drawin’ in a salary now for a fellow what died some forty years ago. You see it happened like this: I had had business deals on with a smug-faced Chinese merchant wot did business at Shanghai. Well, when I was about to sail for the old country, old smug face came to say how sorry he was I was a-goin’ to leave, and hoped he’d have the pleasure of doin’ business with me again when I come back. Well, we started talkin’ and I told him that I should very much like to see a Chinese funeral, and old smug face said that he would gladly oblige me, because a very particular old uncle of his had died and his funeral was shortly to take place. Well, the upshot of it all was that I was invited to go up the river on smug face’s boat to Soochow, where he lived and where his uncle had died, a city some sixty miles away or thereabouts. So there I accordingly went. Have you ever been on one o’ them large sampans, Captain? No? Well, it’s a long sort o’ boat, fitted up very snug indeed, with flowers all trailin’ over the side, and all fixed up to look like an old homestead sailin’ on the river. After a very pleasant trip—and, Lord love you, I did make that old Chinaman laugh tellin’ him things, for I could speak their lingo very well, you understand—well, after a very pleasant trip we gets to Soochow, and a rummy old place it was. It stood right on top of the river, with its old walls runnin’ straight down into the muddy water. It was a strong town and important, a town of fighters and wealthy merchantmen. Well, they was all very pleased to see me and received me very proper. Most of ’em was a-lookin’ over the wall a-wavin’ flags at me, and them as ’adn’t got none were a-wavin’ their pigtails. I might ’ave been the great Cham for all the fuss they made o’ me. O’ course, mind you, I had my enemies. There was a sort o’ lord mayor o’ the place wot I could see didn’t quite approve of me bein’ the nine days’ wonder, but he was one of them self-centred sort o’ coves wot don’t like anyone to have a fling but hisself. But I didn’t mind him, for, although I was only a little fellow, I had an eye like a vulture, a nose like a swordfish, and when I was put out, a way of lashin’ myself about like a tiger’s tail wot used to scare them natives. O’ course, mind you, it wasn’t pleasant when you come to think of it, ’cos there I was the only Englishman amongst them millions of yellow jacks. But an Englishman’s an Englishman all the world over, ain’t he, Captain? and he wants a bit of squashin’, and so that lord mayor discovered, ’cos one day I walked right up to him in the street and I clacked my teeth at him so very loud that he ran home and never annoyed me no more. But I was a-goin’ to tell you about that funeral. When we got to the front door of old smug face’s house we discovered his uncle’s coffin reposin’ upon the doorstep very peaceful but in a most awkward sort of position, ’cos you had to crawl over the blarsted thing to get in or out o’ the door.

===Chinese===
“丧事可以分成三类：有庄严肃穆的丧事，有阴森渗人的丧事，还有滑稽逗乐的丧事。我这辈子见过顶滑稽的一场丧事，是在中国。您知道吗，上校，那边的人难得才下葬一回呢？他们把那该死的棺材撂在地面上不动，四乡八野满地都是这路玩意儿。论丧事办得七零八落，中国可算挂头牌，而且他们那边关于丧事还有旁的种种滑稽章程呢。一个人在那边一旦呜呼了命，要等诸事最后拾掇停当，还得经受一场天大麻烦的折腾。您明白，那边家家户户都供着一位自家专用的祭司，而这种专用祭司，向来是顶狡猾不过的一条老滑狗。死人装进棺材，孝家便付给那条滑狗好大一笔银子，换他向他的神明替他们念顶费劲的祷告。他一走开便接连接祷上几个礼拜，求问他的神明：孝家究竟该把死人葬在哪一处，才好叫他由最便当的一条路进天堂。滑狗祷告的时辰里银子照拿不误，您拿您的假发打赌好啦，他准要装模作样，把那套祷告东拉西扯拖得老长。我还能告诉您，那些中国的祭司老爷，肚里的巧主意着实有一两套。单说一位凌福全（Ling Fu Quong）吧。哼，若不是我——照戏班子里人的说法——给那位爷的小喜剧落了幕，这会儿只怕他还在按月支薪水呢，替一个死了约莫四十年的死人支薪水。事情是这样的：我跟一个满脸油滑相的中国商人有过买卖往来，他在上海（Shanghai）做买卖。好，当我正要扬帆回老家去，老油滑脸赶来道别，说我要走他心里多么难过，盼着我回来时再有幸同我做买卖。我们这么攀谈起来，我说我很想见识见识中国的丧事，老油滑脸说他乐意成全，因为他一位顶要紧的老叔父刚过世，丧事不久便要举办。末了的结局，便是请我搭油滑脸的船溯江而上到苏州（Soochow）去——他住在苏州，他叔父也死在苏州，那城离此约莫六十英里上下。我便如约去了。您坐过那种大舢板（sampan）没有，上校？没坐过？嗐，那是一种长条子的船，里头拾掇得着实安逸，花草藤蔓打两舷垂挂下来，整个儿装点得像一座浮在江上的老家宅园。一路行程畅快极了——老天疼您，我着实把那中国老头儿逗得直乐，讲东讲西给他听，因为他们的乡谈我说得很溜，您明白——好，一路畅快到了苏州，好一座古里古怪的老城。城正骑在江上，老城墙笔直插进浑浊的江水。是座坚固的要紧城池，满城是习武的健儿和殷实的商贾。嗯，他们见了我个个欢喜，接待得极其体面。多半的人扒在城墙上朝我挥旗子，没有旗子的便挥自己的辫子。他们那样大惊小怪地捧我，我倒像是大可汗（the great Cham）驾到了。当然了，您听着，我也有我的对头。当地有一位类似市长大人的人物，我瞧得出，他不大赞成我成了人人称奇的新鲜事儿；可他是那种自命不凡的家伙，见不得旁人出风头，只许他自己。不过我没把他放在心上，因为别看我个子小，我长着一双秃鹫的眼睛、一个剑鱼般的鼻子，发起性子来还能像老虎尾巴似的左右乱甩，专会唬住那班土人。当然了，您听着，细想起来这滋味并不受用，因为放眼成百万的黄皮家伙中间，就我一个英国人。可英国人天底下到哪儿都是英国人，是不是，上校？总得压一压才肯服帖——那位市长大人算是领教了，因为有一天我当街走到他跟前，冲他把牙齿咬得咯咯直响，响得那么厉害，吓得他一溜烟跑回家去，从此再不敢招惹我。可我原是要讲那场丧事来着。到了老油滑脸家的大门口，只见他叔父的棺材安安稳稳停搁在门阶上，位置却别扭到了家，因为不论进出，都得打那该死的玩意儿上头爬过去。

===Original===
“ ‘Lord love you, my most excellent Mipps,’ cried old smug face when he saw it, ‘why, this’ll never do, now will it, for my late lamented uncle’—I forget the uncle’s name but it was Ling something—‘is fairly blocking up the entrance, ain’t it?’”

===Chinese===
“‘老天疼您，我最尊贵的米普斯，’老油滑脸一见便嚷道，‘哎呀，这可万万使不得，是不是？我那令人痛悼的先叔父’——叔父叫什么我忘了，只记得姓凌——‘可把大门口堵了个结结实实，是不是？’”

===Original===
“ ‘Ling Fu Quong,’ I replied, ‘you’ve hit it, for if we ’as to do steeplechase over that there thing every time we wants to get out o’ doors for a breather, well, we’ll fair tire ourselves out.’ And so old smug face agreed, and he accordingly sent for the family sly dog, by which I mean, o’ course, the family parson. Well, old sly dog arrived, and of all the fat, self-satisfied looking bouncers I ever seed, he took the cake. It was easy to see as how he made a good thing out of his job. Well, my old friend smug face begins telling him how awkward it was havin’ a coffin right across the front door, and old sly dog said as how he were very sorry, but it were just in that place wot the gods had told him to put it.

===Chinese===
“‘凌福全，’我答道，‘您可说到点子上了，要是每回想出门透口气，都得来一场跨越那玩意儿的障碍赛，早晚非把人活活累趴下不可。’老油滑脸也认这个账，便打发人去请孝家的老滑狗——我指的是，自然喽，孝家的祭司。好，老滑狗来了，论那种脑满肠肥、顾影自得的大块头，我平生见过的里头数他称第一。一眼便看得出，他这差事捞得着实不坏。嗯，我那位油滑脸老朋友便同他讲，一口棺材正正堵着前门多么碍事，老滑狗说他也着实过意不去，可神明偏偏谕示搁在那儿，他没法子。

===Original===
“ ‘Don’t you think that if we were to offer sacred crackers to the gods that they might find as how they’ve been mistook?’ suggested smug face.

“ ‘I’ll have a try, oh, bereaved one,’ answered sly dog, a-rubbin’ his fat hands with invisible soap, a habit he was very fond of practisin’ and a habit wot always sets my teeth on edge, soap bein’ to my mind such an unnecessary sort o’ institootion.

===Chinese===
“‘依你看，咱们若是给神明供上些神圣的爆竹，他们兴许会发觉自己先前弄岔了？’油滑脸出主意道。

“‘小老儿去试上一试，哦，孝家老爷，’滑狗答道，一面拿看不见的肥皂搓着他那双肥手——这毛病他顶爱使唤，可这毛病一上来总叫我牙根发酸，依我看，肥皂这劳什子压根儿就是多余的家什。

===Original===
“So my old friend unlocks his treasure chest and forks out a regular king’s ransom, which he gives to the sly priest to buy crackers with just to persuade the gods to change their minds. And I tells you that if old sly dog had really spent all that money in crackers, why, Gunpowder Plot wouldn’t have been in it. Anyhow, the priest left us with the money, and we spent the next few days a-climbin’ over that inconvenience whenever we ventured to go out or in doors. You must understand also that coffins out in China ain’t the neat sort of contrivance like we’ve got here. Oh, Lord love you, no, for of all the great cumbersome family coaches I ever seed in the coffin line, them Chinese ones took the cake.

===Chinese===
“于是我那位老朋友开了他的宝贝箱子，掏出一大笔子实打实的国王赎金，交给滑狗祭司去买爆竹，专为了劝神明回心转意。我告诉您，老滑狗若是真把那笔钱统统花在爆竹上，嗐，那阵仗连当年的‘火药阴谋’也得甘拜下风。反正，祭司揣着钱走了，往后几天里，凡大着胆子出出入入，我们都得翻越那件碍事的家伙。您还得明白，中国的棺材可不像咱们这边的那样齐整利落。哎哟我的老天爷，差得远呢，论棺材行里那些笨重得吓人的大家用马车，我平生见过的全都数不上，中国这路货色才称第一。

===Original===
“Well, in a few days back comes the sly dog lookin’ more prosperous than ever. It was very plain to me that he’d been havin’ a good time with that money, and if he had spent five minutes in prayers to his gods I should be very much surprised. Well, he tells my old friend the merchant as how we had to turn out of the house for that night, because the gods had promised to visit him that night if he stayed all alone along of the coffin, and they would then say whether it was possible for the coffin to be moved. So we had to turn out, much to my annoyance, and go to another house wot was owned by a friend of my smug-faced friend. Well, I wasn’t particular about where we stopped, though I could see smug face didn’t like turning out his house, but I felt annoyed to see how very easily he knuckled under to whatever the priest said. So we went away, as I say, for that night. Now the nights come up cold in China, and we both had got two very snivelly noses wot had been brought on by the draughts through not being able to shut that front door. Next morning sly dog came round to say that the gods would visit the house every night and see just where they could order the coffin to be moved to, and in the meantime sly dog was to spend his days and nights in the house, and a very comfortable time he had of it, you may be sure, for my friend the merchant had got a house well stored with very good things.

===Chinese===
“嗐，过了几天，滑狗回来了，瞧着比先前越发红光满面。我心里雪亮：他拿那笔钱过了好几天快活日子，他若真在他那班神明跟前祷告满过五分钟，我才要大大地吃惊呢。嗯，他对我那位商人老朋友说，那一夜我们得腾出宅子，因为神明许下了，若是孝家单独陪着棺材守那一夜，他们便当夜驾临，再谕示那口棺材到底挪得挪不得。于是我们只得搬出去——我心里好不窝火——挪到我那位油滑脸朋友的一个朋友家里去。嗯，住哪儿我倒不挑剔，虽说我瞧得出油滑脸不大情愿腾自己的宅子，可祭司说什么他一听就服软，那副容易劲儿才真叫我来气。就这样，如我所说，那一夜我们躲了出去。要知道，中国的夜里上来就冷，我们俩一个挂着一条涕泪汪汪的鼻子，全拜那扇关不严的前门灌穿堂风所赐。第二天早上，滑狗转回来说，神明夜夜都要驾临那座宅子，亲眼瞧一瞧棺材该谕示挪到哪里去；这期间，宅子便由滑狗日夜住着——他那段日子过得多么受用，您就别提了，我那位商人朋友的宅子里，好东西储存得足足的。

===Original===
“At the end of a week sly dog comes round to say that the gods had decided to move the coffin, and that he had seen their orders carried out. So after giving him more money, much to my indignation, for I couldn’t bear to see my friend imposed upon, we left him and set off for the house. And where do you think that dirty fat priest had put that coffin?”

===Chinese===
“满了一个礼拜，滑狗又转来，说神明已经钦定挪棺材，而且谕旨如何执行，他也亲眼看着办妥了。于是又给了他一笔钱——我心里火得不行，眼见朋友受人愚弄，我实在受不住——我们便辞了他，动身回宅子去。您猜那个龌龊的肥祭司把那口棺材搁到哪儿去了？”

===Original===
“Where?” queried the captain.

===Chinese===
“搁到哪儿了？”上校问道。

===Original===
“Why, in the bed where I was supposed to sleep. Now this really did rouse the devil in me, and I determined to get even with that priest. But I had to think things over very carefully. You see if I objected to sleepin’ in the same bed as the coffin, my friend the smug-faced merchant, who had really been kindness itself to me, might think I thought myself superior to sleepin’ with his uncle, and that I knew would offend him, ’cos the Chinese seem to bear a most ridiculous respect towards their dead relations. So I decided that, come what might, I would certainly sleep there, and at the same time I hit upon a scheme for the undoin’ of that priest.

===Chinese===
“嗐，就搁在我原该睡的那张床上。这一下可真把我肚子里的魔鬼撩拨起来了，我打定主意要同那祭司算这笔账。可我得把事情前前后后想得十分仔细。您想，我若是嫌弃跟棺材同床，我那位油滑脸商人朋友——他待我实在仁至义尽——兴许会当我自视清高，不屑同他的叔父作伴睡；这个我明白，准会伤他的心，因为中国人待过世的亲人，那份敬重简直迂得可笑。所以我拿定主意，天塌下来也一定睡在那儿；与此同时，我琢磨出一条整治那祭司、叫他完蛋的妙计。

===Original===
“Next morning I woke up after a very pleasant sleep alongside that coffin, and felt much refreshed, though o’ course I wasn’t goin’ to let ’em know that. When my friend asked me how I had slept I told him very badly, ’cos all through the night the old uncle in the coffin kept awakin’ up and askin’ if I would go and fetch the priest. So smug face sends round at once to sly dog for me to tell him all about it.

===Chinese===
“第二天早上，我在那口棺材旁边睡了个香甜觉醒来，浑身舒坦；不过这自然不能叫他们知道。朋友问我睡得如何，我说糟透了，因为整夜里，棺材里那位老叔父一个劲儿醒转来，闹着要我走去把祭司请来。于是油滑脸立刻打发人去请滑狗，好叫我当面把这番话讲给他听。

===Original===
“ ‘Did the late lamented uncle of this bereaved man really converse with thee in the night, O Englishman?’ asked the priest, tryin’ to look very knowin’. I was longin’ to reply by givin’ him one in his fat mouth, but I pulled myself together and answered very respectfully:

===Chinese===
“‘这位丧亲之人令人痛悼的先叔父，夜里当真同你交谈过么，哦，英国人？’祭司问道，竭力装出一副无所不知的样子。我满心想回敬他一拳，打在他那张肥嘴上，可我按捺住了，恭恭敬敬地答道：

===Original===
“ ‘Of a truth did the late lamented uncle of this bereaved one’—a-jerkin’ my thumb towards smug face—‘converse with my contemptible self in the small hours of the dawn previous to the inestimable crowing of the invaluable cock upon the temple roof. Of a truth did he converse with me, indeed, and say unto me’—I could speak their lingo very well in those days, I could—‘ “Send for the wise and learned priest of the family and tell him that I have much to say unto him on matters of most heavenly importance, and command him to sleep upon the very spot where thou art now sleeping, O foreigner of the white face. Let him sleep there tomorrow night alone. Let none other be in the house, for it is to the priest alone that I can confide my troubles. Urge also my dutiful nephew to pay large sums of money to the priest so that he may not fail to come to me in my sore and troubled hour.” ’

===Chinese===
“‘千真万确，这位孝家令人痛悼的先叔父’——我把大拇指朝油滑脸那边一翘——‘在天将破晓的时辰、庙顶上那只无价宝鸡啼出它千金难买的第一声之前，同我这卑微之辈交谈过了。千真万确，他实实在在同我交谈了，并且谕示于我’——那时候他们的乡谈我说得着实流利，着实流利——‘“去请孝家那位贤明博学的祭司来，告诉他，我有若干顶顶要紧的天堂大事谕示于他，并命他就睡在你眼下安眠的这块地方，哦，白脸皮的外乡人哪。让他明日独自一人在此安眠。宅中不许再有旁人，因为我的苦楚，唯有祭司一人可以聆听。还要劝我那孝顺的侄儿，付给祭司大笔大笔的银子，好叫他在我痛苦忧烦的时辰，断无不至之理。”’

===Original===
“Well, o’ course they all thought it very wonderful, and, provided with more money by my friend, the priest went off to sleep the night with the coffin. Well, I had pretended to be tired that night and had retired to my sleepin’-room early, so they thought, for we were spendin’ that night with the friends of my friend. But no sooner had I fastened the entrance to my room than I had got out of the window, which looked out upon the city wall, and climbin’ along the parapet I safely reached the ground and set off at a good run to the empty house, gettin’ there well before the priest. Now I had told the merchant to be sure and see the priest safe at the house himself, for I feared that fright might keep the rascal away. The merchant promised to do this, for I believe that by this time he was losin’ confidence in the family confessor. As soon as I got into my old bedroom I opened the coffin, lifted out the corpse, strippin’ him of his funeral clothes, which I donned. Then I hid the corpse in a dark corner of the room behind a screen and got into the great coffin. Now the lids are not screwed down in China, but merely allowed to rest upon the coffin, so I left a very little chink so that I should not have any fear of suffocation. Presently I hears the priest arrive, and my friend bids him goodnight and leaves him. Well, the fellow possessed more courage than I had credited him with, ’cos he comes promptly into the room, counts out his fresh money on the top of the coffin itself, and then curls himself up alongside it upon the mattress. Just as soon as I heard him beginnin’ to breathe heavy I pushed open the coffin lid, callin’ upon him by name in most sepulchral tones. He woke up, o’ course, and sits up on his side of the bed and looked at the coffin; and then he beheld me a-sittin’ up inside the coffin a-lookin’ at him, only, o’ course, he didn’t think it was me, but the dead uncle. Well, he was so frightened that I just had an easy walk over him. I jumped at him, I kicked him, I made him swear that he would return every penny of his false-gotten gains to the merchant, and that if the merchant refused he was to give it to the white stranger that sojourned there, and finally, after thrashing the stuffin’ out of him, I popped him bodily into the coffin, jammed the corpse from behind the screen in on top of him, and over ’em both I closed the lid. Then seein’ as how he was unconscious through the drubbin’ he had had, and the awful fright, I left him and went home to bed at the house of the friends of my friend, gettin’ in as I had got out—through the window. Well, next morning the sly dog turned up and said that the gods had visited him in the night and that the coffin was to be buried twelve feet deep in the merchant’s field, and that he was so overjoyed at having conversed so very pleasantly with the gods that he must insist on returning the gold to the merchant. This the good merchant, of course, refused to accept, so the priest was obliged, according as he had been commanded, to hand it to the white stranger wot sojourned with the merchant and who was your humble servant, Captain. That day I went back to Shanghai loaded with presents, not only from my friend, but from the friends of my friend, at whose house we had sojourned, and with every gold piece out of that sly dog’s pocket, for although a sly dog he certainly was, he was also a cowardly dog, too, and didn’t dare to go against the will of that terrible late lamented uncle of the bereaved one wot was now, and still is, I expect, lying twelve foot down in the field of my inestimable smug-faced merchant who was my friend. And that’s the story of the funniest funeral I was ever at, and there ain’t many wot ever seed a funnier one, I should say.”

===Chinese===
“嗯，不用说，他们都觉得这件事神妙极了，祭司又从我朋友手里领了一笔钱，便去同棺材作伴过夜去了。嗯，那天夜里我装作乏了，早早便退进卧房——他们是这么以为的，因为那晚我们借宿在我朋友的朋友们家里。可我刚把房门闩好，便翻身出了窗户——窗户正对着城墙——顺着墙头的护墙一路爬过去，安安稳稳到了地上，撒腿一溜小跑，奔那座空宅去，赶在祭司前头老早便到了。哦，事先我叮嘱过那位商人，务必亲自把祭司平平安安送进宅子，我只怕这恶棍吓破了胆不敢来。商人答应了，因为我想他到这会儿对家里这位忏悔师也渐渐失了信心。我一进我那间旧卧房，便打开棺材，把尸首抬出来，剥下他那一身殓衣，穿在自己身上；再把尸首藏进屋子黑角里的一架屏风后头，自己钻进那口大棺材。中国的棺材盖不拧螺丝钉，只是虚虚地搭在棺材上，我便留了一条极小的缝，好教我不必担心给活活憋死。不多时，我听见祭司到了，我朋友同他道了晚安便撇下他走了。嗯，这家伙的胆子倒比我估摸的大些，因为他立时进了屋，把新到手的金子一枚一枚点数出来，就点在棺材盖上头，然后往褥子上一蜷，紧挨着棺材躺下了。我一听他呼吸渐渐粗重，立时推开棺材盖，用最阴森的坟茔腔调叫着他的名字。他自然惊醒了，从他那半边床上坐起身，望着棺材；这一望，便望见我正从棺材里坐起来瞅着他——当然喽，他只道那不是我，是死了的叔父。嗐，他吓得那个样子，我简直迈着方步就能从他身上踏过去。我朝他扑过去，我踢他，逼他起誓，要把他那些昧心骗来的钱财一个子儿不少退还商人；商人若是执意不收，便得交给那位寄住在商人家的白脸皮外乡人；末了，把他结结实实一顿好揍，揍得他没了半点人形，我把他整个儿塞进棺材，又把屏风后头那具尸首抽出来压在他身上，给他们两位合上了棺材盖。随后，见他挨了这顿好打、又吃了这场天大的惊吓，昏死过去了，我便撇下他，回到我朋友的朋友们家里睡觉去，进去的路数同出来时一样——还是走窗户。嗐，第二天早上，滑狗露面了，说神明夜里显灵来见过他，棺材要葬进商人的田里，深埋十二英尺；又说他同神明谈得那样投契，欢喜得了不得，执意要把金子退还商人。好商人自然执意不收，于是祭司只得遵照谕示，把金子交给那位同商人住在一处的外乡人——也就是您卑贱的仆人，上校。当天我便回上海去了，满载着礼物——不止我朋友送的，还有我朋友的朋友们（我们借宿在他家）送的——外加那条滑狗兜里的每一枚金币；因为滑狗固然是滑狗，到底也是条胆小狗，断不敢违拗那位可怕已极、令人痛悼的先叔父的意旨。这位叔父，我琢磨着如今还照旧躺在那位无可估量的油滑脸商人——我那位好朋友——的田里，深埋十二英尺呢。这就是我平生亲历的最滑稽的一场丧事；依我看，见过比这更滑稽的丧事的人，怕也为数不多。”

===Original===
“I should think not,” said the captain, and filling their glasses once more they pledged each other, and the captain left the sexton to his hammering, and walked out over the Marsh. He had taken good stock of that coffin shop while Mr. Mipps had been chatting, and he was putting two and two together, and the result was four black marks against the sexton, for he knew him to be out of his own mouth an adventurer, and, when it came to the push, an unscrupulous one. Also he had confessed to having had dealings with buccaneers, and the captain was quick enough to see that he must have been hand in glove with the ringleaders, probably a ringleader himself, a man of the stamp of England and Clegg. Then he had counted no less than thirteen coffins—finished coffins with closed lids—in the shop, and he knew that there were only two bodies awaiting burial in the place—the doctor, Sennacherib Pepper, and the sailor killed at the vicarage. Therefore, what were the others for? That they were misfits was out of the question, for Mipps was too shrewd a man to make eleven misfits; besides, he would have broken them up for fresh material. No, those eleven coffins were destined for other things besides corpses. And the fourth black mark against the sexton was his terrible hypocrisy and the ready wit that hid it. If any man was interested and deeply interested in the great smuggling scheme of Romney Marsh he felt that Mipps was the man, the man-tool of another’s brain, another man mightier than Mipps—the squire probably, Doctor Syn possibly, though he had yet to bring the test to bear upon that curious and enigmatical vicar. But although as yet Doctor Syn was beyond his mental grasp, the Sexton Mipps was within it. He knew that he could make him victim, he was, in fact, sure of his guilt, and, knowing all he did of the man’s character, he fell to wondering how it had been possible for him to fall under the spell of his fascination, for apart from Doctor Syn, whose personality had strongly appealed to him, he would rather have had Mipps for his friend than the rest of the village put together, for that odd little man had a rare way of making you like him, for over all his astute cunning hung a veil, an imperceptible something, that was nearly if not altogether lovable. But the hunter goes to no pains to rehearse the beauty of an animal he is stalking, and the captain knew that as soon as he was ready to strike, no amount of personal fascination possessed by any criminal that he was after would stay his hand when the crucial moment came to destroy, and so he puzzled out his plans for cornering not only Mipps but every wrong ’un on the Marsh, and if the squire and Doctor Syn were in the bag—well, so much the better for the bag.

===Chinese===
“想必不会有，”上校说。两人又把杯子斟满，互相碰盏祝了酒；上校便撇下司事，任他去敲他的锤子，自己迈步走过湿地去了。米普斯先生闲聊的工夫，他把那间棺材铺里的情形细细打量了一遍，心里正把二加二往一处凑，凑出来的结果是：给司事记下了四笔黑账。头一笔，此人凭亲口所言便是个冒险家，而且真到要紧关头，还是个不择手段的冒险家。再者，他自己招认同海盗有过往来，上校眼快，立刻看穿：此人必是同那些为首的恶棍亲密无间，没准儿自己便是头目之一，同英格兰、克莱格一流的人物是一路货色。还有，他数过了，铺子里的成品棺材足足十三口——盖子都已合拢——而他知道，这地方等着下葬的尸首只有两具：医生西拿基立·佩珀，还有在牧师宅送命的那个水手。那么，其余十一口是派什么用场的？说是尺寸不合、做糟了的，断无此理：米普斯那样精明的人，决不会一连做糟十一口；再说，做糟了他也会拆了改作新料。不会错的，那十一口棺材，除了装尸首，另有别的用处。给司事记的第四笔黑账，是他那副可怕的伪善嘴脸，和把伪善遮掩得严丝合缝的那份机灵。若是有人同罗姆尼湿地这桩大走私勾当有利害相干、而且干系极深，他觉得此人必是米普斯：替另一颗头脑使唤的工具人，那颗头脑比米普斯更有力量——多半是那位乡绅，或许还是辛医生——虽然对那位古怪而莫测的牧师，他还有待把考验使将上去。然而，辛医生虽然眼下还逃出他的心想之外，司事米普斯却在他掌握之中。他知道能拿此人做牺牲品；说实在的，他已断定其人有罪；而把这个人的脾性摸得这样透，他不由暗自纳罕：自己先前怎么竟会着了这个人的迷。原来撇开辛医生不谈——辛医生的人格气度对他有极强的吸引力——其余全村人捆作一堆，也不及一个米普斯配做他的朋友；那个古怪的小个子有种稀罕的本事，叫人不能不喜欢他：在他那副精明狡狯之上，笼罩着一层薄纱，一种难以察觉的什么，若非全然可爱，也几乎近于可爱。然而猎人决计不肯费神去称道他正盯梢的野兽如何俊美；上校心里明白，只等他准备出手，他要缉拿的任何罪犯，纵有千般迷人的能耐，到了摧毁的紧要关头，也休想按住他的手。于是他苦苦盘算他的计划：要把米普斯，连同湿地上每一个歪货，一并逼进绝境；若是乡绅和辛医生也落进袋里——嗐，那只袋子就愈发丰满了。

## XXVI. The Devil's Tiring House / 二十六　魔鬼的化妆室

===Original===
If the village was abed by ten o'clock, the coffin shop was very much alive at half an hour after midnight. Jerk, according to his instructions, found himself tapping upon the back window at that very hour and immediately found himself hauled into the house by Mr. Mipps himself. The sexton wore a voluminous riding cloak, heavily tippeted, and a black mask hid the upper part of his face, but Jerk could see by a glance at the fine sharp jaws that Mipps had laid aside his oiliness of manner, his sarcastic wit, and cringing self-complacency, and was allowing the real man that was in him to shine forth for once in a way probably for his express advantage.
